\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\tilde{n}}}\left(\frac{2\tilde{n}}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})}-\frac{2\tilde{n}}{(p^{\prime}(n)+\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}+4\log({k}n))}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\tilde{n}}}\cdot\frac{4\tilde{n}\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}+8\tilde{n}\log(kn)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{n}}\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}+2\sqrt{\tilde{n}}\log(kn)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)},

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}+2\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}\log(kn)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)}.

The last inequality works on event
\mathcal{E}_{v}
, by which we have
\tilde{n}\leq\frac{2(C_{1}+1)n}{C_{1}k}
. Then,
p^{\prime}(n)>Ck^{3}n\log(kn)
gives us

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}\sqrt{4/(Ck^{3}n)}p^{\prime}(n)+\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}(2/Ck^{3}n)p^{\prime}(n)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(1/(C_{2}\sqrt{k^{4}}))+(1/(C_{3}k^{3.5}\sqrt{n}))}{p^{\prime}(n)(1-\sqrt{4/(Ck^{3}n)})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{4}+1}{C_{4}}\cdot\frac{1}{k^{2}p},

where in the last step we use the fact that
C,C_{2},C_{3}>1
are large enough.
To lower bound
g_{ij}^{(0)}
, we have with probability at least
1-\frac{2}{k^{2}n^{2}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\tilde{n}}}\left(\frac{2\tilde{n}}{(p^{\prime}(n)+\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}+4\log(kn))}-\frac{2\tilde{n}}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4\sqrt{\tilde{n}}}\cdot\frac{-4\tilde{n}\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}-8\tilde{n}\log(kn)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\sqrt{\tilde{n}}\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}+2\sqrt{\tilde{n}}\log(kn)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)},

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)}+2\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}\log(kn)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)}.

Because
p^{\prime}(n)>Ck^{3}n\log(kn)
, we get

\displaystyle\mathbf{e}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}\sqrt{4/(Ck^{3}n)}p^{\prime}(n)+\sqrt{(1+1/C_{1})n/k}(2/Ck^{3}n)p^{\prime}(n)}{(p^{\prime}(n)-\sqrt{4\log({k}n)p^{\prime}(n)})p^{\prime}(n)}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{(1/(C_{2}\sqrt{k^{4}}))+(1/(C_{3}k^{3.5}\sqrt{n}))}{p^{\prime}(n)(1-\sqrt{4/(Ck^{3}n)})}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{C_{4}+1}{C_{4}}\cdot\frac{1}{k^{2}p},

where in the last step we use the fact that
C,C_{2},C_{3}>1
are large enough. We complete the proof by applying a union bounds over all
k
classes and
n
training examples.

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
3

In this section, we provide the proof of Theorem
3
, which was discussed in Section
3.2.2
.
After having derived the interpolation condition in Equation (
12
) for multiclass SVM,
the proofs is in fact a rather simple extension of the arguments provided in
[
MNS+21
,
HMX21
]
to the multiclass case. This is unlike the GMM case that we considered in Section
6.2
, which required substantial additional effort over and above the binary case
[
WT21
]
.

For this section, we define
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X}
as shorthand (we denoted the same quantity as
\mathbf{A}_{k}
in Section
6.2
).
Recall that the eigendecomposition of the covariance matrix is given by
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{T}=\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}
.
By rotation invariance of the standard normal variable, we can write
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{Q}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}
, where the entries of
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}
are IID
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
random variables.
Finally, recall that we denoted
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\cdots&\lambda_{p}\end{bmatrix}
and defined the effective dimensions
d_{2}=\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{1}^{2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{2}^{2}}
and
d_{\infty}=\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{1}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{\infty}}
.
Observe that Equation (
12
) in Theorem
1
is equivalent to the condition

\displaystyle z_{ci}\mathbf{e}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{c}>0,~~\text{for \ all}\ \ c\in[k]~~\text{and}~~i\in[n].

(61)

We fix
c\in[k]
and drop the subscript
c
, using
\overline{\mathbf{z}}
to denote the vector
\mathbf{z}_{c}
.
We first provide a deterministic equivalence to Equation (
12
) that resembles the condition provided in
[
HMX21
, Lemma 1]
. Our proof is slightly modified compared to
[
HMX21
, Lemma 1]
and relies on elementary use of block matrix inversion identity.

Lemma 11
.

Let
\mathbf{Q}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times n}=[\mathbf{q}_{1},\cdots,\mathbf{q}_{n}]
. In our notation, Equation (
12
) holds for a fixed
c
if and only if:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{z_{i}}\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus i}^{T}\Big(\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}\Big)^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{q}_{i}<1,~~\text{for all}~~i=1,\cdots,n.

(62)

Above,
\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus i}\in\mathbb{R}^{(n-1)\times 1}
is obtained by removing the
i
-th entry from vector
\overline{\mathbf{z}}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times(n-1)}
is obtained by removing the
i
-th column from
\mathbf{Q}
.

Proof.

By symmetry, it suffices to consider the case
i=1
. We first write

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{q}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{q}_{1}&\mathbf{q}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}^{T}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{q}_{1}&\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}^{T}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}\end{bmatrix}\triangleq\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&\mathbf{b}^{T}\\
\mathbf{b}&\mathbf{D}\end{bmatrix}.

By Schur complement
[
Ber09
]
, we have

\mathbf{A}\succ\mathbf{0}~\textit{iff}~\text{either}~\left\{\alpha>0~\text{and}~\mathbf{D}-\frac{\mathbf{b}\mathbf{b}^{T}}{\alpha}\succ\mathbf{0}\right\}~\text{or}~\left\{\mathbf{D}\succ\mathbf{0}~\text{and}~\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b}>0\right\}.

Since the entries of
\mathbf{Q}
are drawn from a continuous distribution (IID standard Gaussian), both
\mathbf{A}
and
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}^{T}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}
are positive definite almost surely. Therefore,
\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b}>0
almost surely.

Thus, by block matrix inversion identity
[
Ber09
]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}^{-1}=\begin{bmatrix}(\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{-1}&-(\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{-1}\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\\
-\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b}(\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{-1}&\mathbf{D}^{-1}+\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b}(\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{-1}\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Therefore,
\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}=(\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}1&-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Hence we have

\displaystyle z_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{z}}=(\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{-1}(z_{1}^{2}-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}(z_{1}\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus 1})),

where we use the fact that
\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{1}=z_{1}
. Since
\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b}>0
almost surely, we have

\displaystyle z_{1}\mathbf{e}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{z}}>0

\displaystyle\iff(\alpha-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\mathbf{b})^{-1}(z_{1}^{2}-\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}(z_{1}\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus 1}))>0

\displaystyle\iff\frac{1}{z_{1}}\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{D}^{-1}\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus 1}<1.

Recall that
\mathbf{b}^{T}=\mathbf{q}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}
and
\mathbf{D}=\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}^{T}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus 1}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Next, we define the following events:

1.

For
i\in[n]
,
\mathcal{B}_{i}:=\Big\{\frac{1}{z_{i}}\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{\setminus i}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{q}_{i}\geq 1\Big\}
.

2.

For
i\in[n]
, given
t>0
,
\mathcal{E}_{i}(t):=\Big\{\|(\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{\setminus i}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})^{T}\|_{2}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{t}\Big\}
.

3.

\mathcal{B}:=\cup_{i=1}^{n}\mathcal{B}_{i}
.

We know all the data points are support vectors i.e. Equation (
61
) holds, if none of the events
\mathcal{B}_{i}
happens; hence,
\mathcal{B}
is the undesired event. We want to upper bound the probability of event
\mathcal{B}
.
As in the argument provided in
[
HMX21
]
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B})\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{i}|\mathcal{E}_{i}(t)^{c})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{i}(t))\Big).

(63)

The lemma below gives an upper bound on
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{i}|\mathcal{E}_{i}(t)^{c})
.

Lemma 12
.

For any
t>0
,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{i}|\mathcal{E}_{i}(t)^{c})\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t}{2ck^{2}}\right)
.

Proof.

On the event
\mathcal{E}_{i}(t)^{c}
, we have
\|(\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{\setminus i}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})^{T}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{t}
. Since, by its definition,
|\frac{1}{z_{i}}|\leq k
, we have
\frac{1}{z_{i}}\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{\setminus i}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{q}_{i}
is conditionally sub-Gaussian
[
Wai19
, Chapter 2]
with parameter at most
ck^{2}\|(\overline{\mathbf{z}}_{\setminus i}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{\setminus i}^{-1}\mathbf{Q}_{\setminus i}^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda})^{T}\|_{2}^{2}\leq ck^{2}/t
. Then the sub-Gaussian tail bound gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{B}_{i}|\mathcal{E}_{i}(t)^{c})\leq 2\exp\left(-\frac{t}{2ck^{2}}\right),

(64)

which completes the prof.
∎

Next we upper bound
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{i}(t))
with
t=d_{\infty}/(2n)
. Since
\|\mathbf{z}_{\setminus i}\|_{2}\leq\|\mathbf{y}_{\setminus i}\|_{2}
, we can directly use
[
HMX21
, Lemma 4]
.

Lemma 13

(Lemma 4,
[
HMX21
]
)
.

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathcal{E}_{i}\left(\frac{d_{\infty}}{2n}\right)\right)\leq 2\cdot 9^{n-1}\cdot\exp\left(-c_{1}\min\left\{\frac{d_{2}}{4c^{2}},\frac{d_{\infty}}{c}\right\}\right)
.

The results above are proved for fixed choices of
i\in[n]
and
c\in[k]
.
We combine Lemmas
12
and
13
with a union bound over all
n
training examples and
k
classes to upper bound the probability of the undesirable event
\mathcal{B}
over all
k
classes by:

\displaystyle kn9^{n-1}\cdot\exp\left(-c_{1}\min\left\{\frac{d_{2}}{4c^{2}},\frac{d_{\infty}}{c}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-c_{1}\min\left\{\frac{d_{2}}{4c^{2}},\frac{d_{\infty}}{c}\right\}+C_{1}\log(kn)+C_{2}n\right)

\displaystyle\text{and}~2kn\cdot\exp\left(-\frac{d_{\infty}}{2ck^{2}n}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(-\frac{c_{2}d_{\infty}}{ck^{2}n}+C_{3}\log(kn)\right).

Thus, the probability that every data point is a support vector is at least

\displaystyle 1-\exp\left(-c_{1}\min\left\{\frac{d_{2}}{4c^{2}},\frac{d_{\infty}}{c}\right\}+C_{1}\log(kn)+C_{2}n\right)-\exp\left(-\frac{c_{2}d_{\infty}}{ck^{2}n}+C_{3}\log(kn)\right).

To ensure that
\exp\left(-c_{1}\min\left\{\frac{d_{2}}{4c^{2}},\frac{d_{\infty}}{c}\right\}+C_{1}\log(kn)+C_{2}n\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{c_{2}d_{\infty}}{ck^{2}n}+C_{3}\log(kn)\right)\leq\frac{c_{4}}{n}
, we consider the conditions
c_{1}\min\left\{\frac{d_{2}}{4c^{2}},\frac{d_{\infty}}{c}\right\}-C_{1}\log(kn)-C_{2}n\geq\log(n)
and
\frac{c_{2}d_{\infty}}{ck^{2}n}-C_{3}\log(kn)\geq\log(n)
to be satisfied.
These are equivalent to the conditions provided in Equation (
17
).
This completes the proof.
Note that throughout the proof, we did not use any generative model assumptions on the labels given the covariates, so in fact our proof applies to scenarios beyond the MLM.
∎

Appendix C
Classification error proofs for GMM

In this section, we provide the proofs of classification error under the GMM (Theorem
4
and Theorem
6
).

C.1
Proof of Theorem
4

C.1.1
Proof strategy and notations

The notation and main arguments of this proof follow closely the content of Section
6.2
.

Our starting point here is the lemma below (adapted from
[
TOS20
, D.10]
) that provides a simpler upper bound on the class-wise error
\mathbb{P}_{e|c}
.

Lemma 14
.

Under GMM,
\mathbb{P}_{e|c}\leq\sum_{j\neq c}Q\left(\frac{(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}{\|{\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}}\|_{2}}\right)
. In particular, if
(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}>0
, then
\mathbb{P}_{e|c}\leq\sum_{j\neq c}\exp\left(-\frac{((\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c})^{2}}{4(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}+\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})}\right)
.

Proof.

[
TOS20
, D.10]
shows
\mathbb{P}_{e|c}
is upper bounded by
\sum_{j\neq c}Q\left(\frac{(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}{\|{\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}}\|_{2}}\right)
. Then if
(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}>0
, the Chernoff bound
[
Wai19
, Ch. 2]
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e|c}\leq\sum_{j\neq c}\exp\left(-\frac{((\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c})^{2}}{2\|{\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}}\|_{2}^{2}}\right)\leq\sum_{j\neq c}\exp\left(-\frac{((\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c})^{2}}{4(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}+\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})}\right),

where the last inequality uses the identity
\mathbf{a}^{T}\mathbf{b}\leq 2(\mathbf{a}^{T}\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}^{T}\mathbf{b})
.
∎

Thanks to Lemma
14
, we can upper bound
P_{e|c}
by lower bounding the terms

\displaystyle\frac{((\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c})^{2}}{(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}+\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})},~~\text{for~all}~~c\neq j\in[k].

(65)

Our key observation is that this can be accomplished without the need to control the more intricate cross-correlation terms
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}
for
c\neq j\in[k].

Without loss of generality, we assume onwards that
c=k
and
j=k-1
(as in Section
6.2
).
Similar to Section
6.2
, the quadratic forms introduced in Equation (
40
) play key role here, as well. For convenience, we recall the definitions of the
c
-th order quadratic forms
for
c,j,m\in[k]
and
i\in[n]
:

\displaystyle s_{mj}^{(c)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j},

\displaystyle t_{mj}^{(c)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{d}_{m}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{j},

\displaystyle h_{mj}^{(c)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{j},

\displaystyle g_{ji}^{(c)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{i},

\displaystyle f_{ji}^{(c)}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{d}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{c}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{i}.

Further, recall that
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}=\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
and
\mathbf{X}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}+\mathbf{Q}
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}&=\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}+\sum_{m\neq c}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}+\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}\text{ and }\\
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}&=\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}+\sum_{m\neq c,j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}+\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}.\end{split}

(66)

Additionally,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}=\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c},~~\text{and}~~\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}.

To lower bound
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}
, we first focus on the dominant terms of Equation (
66
),

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}+\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}-\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}.

(67)

The above terms dominate the bound because, according to Assumption
2
, the inner products between different mean vectors are small compared to the norms of mean vectors.

We now lower bound Equation (
67
) divided by
(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}+\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})
.
Using the leave-one-out trick in Section
6.2
and the matrix-inversion lemma, we show in Appendix
C.1.5
that

\displaystyle\frac{\eqref{eq:pfgmmrisk01}}{(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}+\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})}=\frac{D_{1}}{D_{2}},

(68)

\displaystyle D_{1}=\left(\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{cc}^{(j)}-s_{cc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}+{h_{cc}^{(j)}}^{2}+h_{cc}^{(j)}-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{jc}^{(j)}-h_{jc}^{(j)}-h_{jc}^{(j)}h_{cc}^{(j)}+s_{jc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle D_{2}=\left(\frac{s_{cc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}}+\frac{s_{jj}^{(-j)}}{\det_{-j}}\right),

where
\det_{j}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{cc}^{(j)})s_{cc}^{(j)}+({h_{cc}^{(j)}}+1)^{2}
. Note that
\det_{j}=\det_{-c}
when
c=k
and
j=k-1
.

Next, we will prove that

\displaystyle\eqref{eq:lowerquadratio}\geq\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\frac{\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{2}n}{p}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}-C_{3}\min\{\sqrt{k},\sqrt{\log(2n)}\}\right)^{2}}{C_{6}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{kp}{n}\right)}\,.

(69)

C.1.2
Proof of Equation (
69
)

We will lower bound the numerator and upper bound the denominator of Equation (
68
).
We will work on the high-probability event
\mathcal{E}_{v}
defined in Equation (
50
) in Appendix
A.1
.
For quadratic forms such as
s_{cc}^{(j)},t_{cc}^{(j)}
and
h_{cc}^{(j)}
, the Gram matrix
\mathbf{A}_{j}^{-1}
does not “include” the
c
-th mean component because we have fixed
c=k,j=k-1
.
Thus, we can directly apply Lemma
2
to get

\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}-1}{C_{1}}\cdot\frac{n}{kp}\leq

\displaystyle s_{cc}^{(j)}\leq\frac{C_{1}+1}{C_{1}}\cdot\frac{n}{kp},

\displaystyle t_{cc}^{(j)}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{2}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}}{p},

\displaystyle-\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\frac{C_{3}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{k}p}\leq

\displaystyle h_{cc}^{(j)}\leq\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\frac{C_{3}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{k}p},

on the event
\mathcal{E}_{v}
.
We need some additional work to bound
s_{jc}^{(j)}=\mathbf{v}_{j}\mathbf{A}_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
and
h_{jc}^{(j)}=\mathbf{v}_{j}\mathbf{A}_{j}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}
, since the Gram matrix
\mathbf{A}_{j}^{-1}
“includes”
\mathbf{v}_{j}
.
The proof here follows the machinery introduced in Appendix
A.2
for proving Lemma
2
. We provide the core argument and refer the reader therein for additional justifications. By Equation (
102
) in Appendix
F.1
(with the index
j-1
replacing the index
0
), we first have

\displaystyle s_{jc}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=s_{jc}^{(j-1)}-\frac{1}{\det_{j-1}}(\star)_{s}^{(j-1)},

where we define

(\star)_{s}^{(j-1)}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{jj}^{(j-1)})s_{jj}^{(j-1)}s_{jc}^{(j-1)}+s_{jc}^{(j-1)}{h_{jj}^{(j-1)}}^{2}+s_{jc}^{(j-1)}h_{jj}^{(j-1)}+s_{jj}^{(j-1)}h_{jc}^{(j-1)},

and
\det_{j-1}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{jj}^{(j-1)})s_{jj}^{(j-1)}+({h_{jj}^{(j-1)}}+1)^{2}
. Further, we have

\displaystyle|s_{jc}^{(j)}|

\displaystyle=\left|\left(1-\frac{(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{jj}^{(j-1)})s_{jj}^{(j-1)}+{h_{jj}^{(j-1)}}^{2}}{\det_{j-1}}\right)s_{jc}^{(j-1)}-\frac{1}{\det_{j-1}}(s_{jc}^{(j-1)}h_{jj}^{(j-1)}+s_{jj}^{(j-1)}h_{jc}^{(j-1)})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C}|s_{jc}^{(j-1)}|+\frac{1}{\det_{j-1}}|(s_{jc}^{(j-1)}h_{jj}^{(j-1)}+s_{jj}^{(j-1)}h_{jc}^{(j-1)})|.

We focus on the dominant term
|s_{jj}^{(j-1)}h_{jc}^{(j-1)}|
.
Using a similar argument to that provided in Appendix
A.2
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{|s_{jj}^{(j-1)}h_{jc}^{(j-1)}|}{\det_{j-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|s_{jj}^{(j-1)}h_{jc}^{(j-1)}|}{(1+{h_{jj}^{(j-1)}})^{2}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\left(1-\frac{C_{2}\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\epsilon_{n}}{k^{2}\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{n}{kp}\cdot\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\epsilon_{n}}{k^{2}\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{3}\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\epsilon_{n}}{\left(1-\frac{C_{2}\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\epsilon_{n}}{k^{2}\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}k^{2}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{kp}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle|s_{jc}^{(j-1)}|\leq\frac{C_{4}+1}{C_{4}}\cdot\frac{\sqrt{n}}{kp}.

Similarly, we bound the remaining term
h_{jc}^{(j)}
.
Specifically, by Equation (
104
) in Section
F.1
, we have

\displaystyle h_{jc}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=h_{jc}^{(j-1)}-\frac{1}{\det_{j-1}}(\star)_{h}^{(j-1)},

where we define

(\star)_{h}^{(j-1)}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{jj}^{(j-1)})s_{jj}^{(j-1)}h_{jc}^{(j-1)}+h_{jc}^{(j-1)}{h_{jj}^{(j-1)}}^{2}+h_{jc}^{(j-1)}h_{jj}^{(j-1)}+s_{jj}^{(j-1)}t_{jc}^{(j-1)}.

Furthermore,

\displaystyle|h_{jc}^{(j)}|

\displaystyle=\left|\left(1-\frac{(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{jj}^{(j-1)})s_{jj}^{(j-1)}+{h_{jj}^{(j-1)}}^{2}}{\det_{j-1}}\right)h_{jc}^{(j-1)}-\frac{1}{\det_{j-1}}(h_{jc}^{(j-1)}h_{jj}^{(j-1)}+s_{jj}^{(j-1)}t_{jc}^{(j-1)})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{C}|h_{jc}^{(j-1)}|+\frac{1}{\det_{j-1}}|(h_{jc}^{(j-1)}h_{jj}^{(j-1)}+s_{jj}^{(j-1)}t_{jc}^{(j-1)})|.

We again consider the dominant term
|s_{jj}^{(j-1)}t_{jc}^{(j-1)}|/\det_{j-1}
and get

\displaystyle\frac{|s_{jj}^{(j-1)}t_{jc}^{(j-1)}|}{\det_{j-1}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|s_{jj}^{(j-1)}t_{jc}^{(j-1)}|}{(1+{h_{jj}^{(j-1)}})^{2}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\left(1-\frac{C_{2}\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\epsilon_{n}}{k^{2}\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}}\cdot\frac{n}{kp}\cdot\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}}{p}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{3}\epsilon_{n}}{\left(1-\frac{C_{2}\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\epsilon_{n}}{k^{1.5}\sqrt{n}}\right)^{2}k^{2}\sqrt{n}}\cdot\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{k}p}.

Thus, we find that

\displaystyle|h_{jc}^{(j-1)}|\leq\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}\frac{C_{4}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{k}p}.

We are now ready to lower bound the RHS in Equation (
68
) by lower bounding its numerator and upper bounding its denominator.

First, for the numerator we have the following sequence of inequalities:

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{cc}^{(j)}-s_{cc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}+{h_{cc}^{(j)}}^{2}+h_{cc}^{(j)}-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{jc}^{(j)}-h_{jc}^{(j)}-h_{jc}^{(j)}h_{cc}^{(j)}+s_{jc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{cc}^{(j)}-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{jc}^{(j)}-s_{cc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}+s_{jc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}+h_{cc}^{(j)}-h_{jc}^{(j)}-h_{jc}^{(j)}h_{cc}^{(j)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}-1}{C_{1}}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}n}{kp}-\frac{C_{2}+1}{C_{2}}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\sqrt{n}}{kp}-\frac{C_{3}n}{p}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}n}{kp}-\frac{C_{4}n}{p}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\sqrt{n}}{kp}-\frac{C_{5}\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{k}p}.

Above, we use the fact that the terms
|h_{cc}^{(j)}|,|h_{jc}^{(j)}|\leq C\epsilon/(k^{2}\sqrt{n})
are sufficiently small compared to
1
.
Consequently, the numerator is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\left(\frac{C_{1}-1}{C_{1}}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}n}{kp}-\frac{C_{2}+1}{C_{2}}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\sqrt{n}}{kp}-\frac{C_{3}n}{p}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}n}{kp}-\frac{C_{4}n}{p}\cdot\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\sqrt{n}}{kp}-\frac{C_{5}\tilde{\rho}_{n,k}n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}{\sqrt{k}p}\right)^{2}\Big/\text{det}_{j}^{2}.

(70)

Second, we upper bound the denominator. For this, note that under the assumption of nearly equal energy and equal priors on class means (Assumption
1
), there exist constants
C_{1},C_{2}>0
such that
C_{1}\leq\det_{j}/\det_{-j}\leq C_{2}
.
(In fact, a very similar statement was proved in Equation (
45
) and used in the proof of Theorem
2
).
Moreover, Lemma
2
shows that the terms
s_{cc}^{(j)}
and
s_{jj}^{(-j)}
are of the same order, so it suffices to upper bound
\frac{s_{cc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}}
.
Again applying Lemma
2
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{s_{cc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}}\leq\frac{C_{6}}{\det_{j}}\cdot\frac{n}{kp}\,

(71)

on the event
\mathcal{E}_{v}
.
Then, combining Equations (
70
) and (
71
) gives us

(
68
)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n}{C_{0}kp}\cdot\frac{1}{\det_{j}}\left((1-\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{2}n}{p})\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}-C_{3}\min\{\sqrt{k},\sqrt{\log(2n)}\}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n}{C_{0}kp}\cdot\frac{1}{\frac{C_{4}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}n}{kp}+2+\frac{C_{5}n^{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}}{kp^{2}}}\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{2}n}{p}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}-C_{3}\min\{\sqrt{k},\sqrt{\log(2n)}\}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\frac{\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{2}n}{p}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}-C_{3}\min\{\sqrt{k},\sqrt{\log(2n)}\}\right)^{2}}{C_{6}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{kp}{n}\right)},

(72)

where the second inequality follows from the following upper bound on
\det_{j}
on the event
\mathcal{E}_{v}
:

\displaystyle\text{det}_{j}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{cc}^{(j)})s_{cc}^{(j)}+({h_{cc}^{(j)}}+1)^{2}\leq\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{cc}^{(j)}+2({h_{cc}^{(j)}}^{2}+1)\leq\frac{C_{4}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}n}{kp}+2+\frac{C_{5}n^{2}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}}{kp^{2}}.

C.1.3
Bounding the remaining terms in (
66
)

The previous sections of the proof bounded the dominant terms in Equation (
66
). Now, we turn to bounding the remaining terms
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
and
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}
. Under the nearly equal energy and priors assumption, the
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}
terms have the same bound for every
j\in[k]
except for some constants. Similarly, the
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{m}
terms also have the same bound for all
j\neq m\in[k]
except for some constants.
An upper bound on classification error can then be derived in terms of the inner products between the mean vectors. Specifically, we need to include the bounds of
\sum_{m\neq c}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
,
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}
and
\sum_{m\neq c,j}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}
. Recall that in Appendix
C.1.4
we show that
\mathbf{v}_{c}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}={(s_{cj}^{(j)}+s_{cj}^{(j)}h_{cc}^{(j)}-s_{cc}^{(j)}h_{jc}^{(j)})}/{\det_{j}}
and
\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}=s_{jj}^{(-j)}/{\det_{-j}}
. We also show that the bound for
{s_{cc}^{(j)}}
(also
{s_{jj}^{(-j)}}
) is at the order of
\mathcal{O}(n/(kp))
and the bound for
s_{cj}^{(j)}
is at the order of
\mathcal{O}(\sqrt{n}/(kp))
when
c\neq j
, which is significantly smaller than
\mathcal{O}(n/(kp))
when
n
is large. Additionally, the bound for
|h_{jc}^{(j)}|
is sufficiently small. Combining these results and the assumption of mutually incoherent means, we can see that the bounds for these additional terms included are still much smaller than the bound of
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
, which is the dominant term. Therefore, they will not change the generalization bound of (
68
) except up to constant factors.

C.1.4
Completing the proof

Because of our assumption of nearly equal energy on class means and equal priors, the analysis above can be applied to bound
\frac{((\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c})^{2}}{(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}+\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})}
, for every
j\neq c
and
c\in[k]
.
We define the
unconditional
event

\mathcal{E}_{u2}:=\left\{\frac{((\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c})^{2}}{(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}+\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j})}\text{ is lower bounded by}~\eqref{eq:lowerquadratio02}~\text{for every}~j\neq c\right\}.

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{u2}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{u2}^{c}|\mathcal{E}_{v})+\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{v}^{c})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{4}}{n}+c_{5}k(e^{-\frac{n}{c_{6}}}+e^{-\frac{n}{c_{7}k^{2}}})\leq\frac{c_{4}}{n}+c_{8}ke^{-\frac{n}{c_{7}k^{2}}}

for constants
c_{i}
’s
>1
. Thus, the class-wise error
\mathbb{P}_{e|c}
is upper bounded by

\displaystyle(k-1)\exp{\left(-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\frac{\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{1}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{2}n}{p}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}-C_{3}\min\{\sqrt{k},\sqrt{\log(2n)}\}\right)^{2}}{C_{4}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{kp}{n}\right)}\right)}

with probability at least
1-\frac{c_{4}}{n}-c_{8}ke^{-\frac{n}{c_{7}k^{2}}}
.
This completes the proof.
∎

C.1.5
Proof of Equation (
68
)

Here, using the results of Section
F.1
, we show how to obtain Equation (
68
) from Equation (
65
). First, by
[
WT21
, Appendix C.2]
(with
\mathbf{y}
replaced by
\mathbf{v}_{m}
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{m}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{m}=\frac{s_{mm}^{(-m)}}{\det_{-m}},~~\text{for~all}~~m\in[k],

where
\det_{-m}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{mm}^{(-m)})s_{mm}^{(-m)}+({h_{mm}^{(-m)}}+1)^{2}
. Then
[
WT21
, Equation (44)]
gives

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{v}_{c}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}+\mathbf{v}_{c}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}=\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{cc}^{(j)}-s_{cc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}+{h_{cc}^{(j)}}^{2}+h_{cc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}},

where
\det_{j}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{cc}^{(j)})s_{cc}^{(j)}+({h_{cc}^{(j)}}+1)^{2}
. Note that
\det_{j}=\det_{-c}
when
c=k
and
j=k-1
.

For
\mathbf{v}_{c}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}
and
\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}
, we can again express the
k
-th order quadratic forms in terms of
j
-th order quadratic forms as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{c}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}

\displaystyle=\frac{s_{cj}^{(j)}+s_{cj}^{(j)}h_{cc}^{(j)}-s_{cc}^{(j)}h_{jc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}},

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}

\displaystyle=\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{cc}^{(j)}h_{jc}^{(j)}-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{cj}^{(j)}h_{cc}^{(j)}+h_{cc}^{(j)}h_{jc}^{(j)}+h_{jc}^{(j)}-s_{cj}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}}.

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}\mathbf{v}_{c}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}+\mathbf{v}_{j}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{c}=\frac{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{jc}^{(j)}+h_{jc}^{(j)}+h_{jc}^{(j)}h_{cc}^{(j)}-s_{jc}^{(j)}t_{cc}^{(j)}}{\det_{j}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

C.2
Proof of Theorem
6

In this section we prove Theorem
6
.
The simplex ETF setting for the class means gives us
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}=-(k-1)\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}
for
m\neq c
.
Therefore, following the analysis above, the additional term
\sum_{m\neq c}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{m}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
is upper bounded by
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\max_{m,c}|\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}|
. Since the dominating term in
\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
is
{s_{cj}^{(j)}}
, which has a much smaller upper bound than the dominating term in
\mathbf{v}_{c}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{c}
, and the term
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{j}
in
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}
has a positive contribution to
\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{c}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{j}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{c}
under the simplex ETF setting, the final generalization bound does not change except up to constant factors.

C.3
Proof of Corollary
3

We now prove the condition for benign overfitting provided in Corollary
3
. Note that following Theorem
2
, we assume that

\displaystyle p>C_{1}k^{3}n\log(kn)+n-1\quad\text{ and }\quad p>C_{2}k^{1.5}n^{1.5}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}.

(73)

We begin with the setting where
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}>C\frac{kp}{n},\ \ \text{for some}\ \ C>1.

In this case, we get that Equation (
72
) is lower bounded by

\frac{1}{c}{\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{4}n}{p}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}-C_{5}\sqrt{k}\right)^{2}},

and we have

\displaystyle{\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{4}n}{p}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}-C_{5}\sqrt{k}\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle>\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}-2\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\frac{C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}-2\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\frac{C_{4}n}{p}-2C_{5}\sqrt{k}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}

\displaystyle>\left(1-\frac{2C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{kp}{n}-2\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\frac{C_{4}n}{p}-2C_{5}\sqrt{k}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}.

(74)

Then Equation (
73
) gives

(
74
)

\displaystyle>\left(1-\frac{2C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{kp}{n}-\left(\frac{p}{k^{1.5}n^{1.5}}\right)^{2}\frac{C_{6}n}{p}-\frac{C_{7}\sqrt{k}p}{k^{1.5}n^{1.5}}

\displaystyle=\frac{kp}{n}\left(1-\frac{2C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{6}}{k^{4}n}-\frac{C_{7}}{k^{2}\sqrt{n}}\right),

(75)

which goes to
+\infty
as
\left(\frac{p}{n}\right)\to\infty
.

Next, we consider the case
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{kp}{n}
. Moreover, we assume that
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{4}=C_{2}\left(\frac{p}{n}\right)^{\alpha},
for
\alpha>1
.
Then, Equation (
72
) is lower bounded by
\frac{n}{ckp}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{4}\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{4}n}{p}\right)-\frac{C_{5}\sqrt{k}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}\right)^{2}
, and we get

\displaystyle\frac{n}{kp}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{4}\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{4}n}{p}\right)-\frac{C_{5}\sqrt{k}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle>\left(1-\frac{2C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{n}{kp}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{4}-\frac{C_{6}n^{2}}{kp^{2}}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{4}-\frac{C_{7}n}{\sqrt{k}p}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{3}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{2C_{3}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{1}{k}\left(\frac{p}{n}\right)^{\alpha-1}-\frac{C_{6}}{k}\left(\frac{p}{n}\right)^{\alpha-2}-\frac{C_{7}}{\sqrt{k}}\left(\frac{p}{n}\right)^{0.75\alpha-1},

(76)

where the last inequality uses Equations (
73
) and condition
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{kp}{n}
.
Consequently, the RHS of Equation (
76
) will go to
+\infty
as
\left(\frac{p}{n}\right)\to\infty
, provided that
\alpha>1
. Overall, it suffices to have

\displaystyle p>\max\left\{C_{1}k^{3}n\log(kn)+n-1,C_{2}k^{1.5}n^{1.5}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2},\frac{n\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}}{k}\right\},

and

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{4}\geq C_{8}\left(\frac{p}{n}\right)^{\alpha},\ \ \text{for}\ \alpha\in(1,2].

All of these inequalities hold provided that
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}=\Theta(p^{\beta})
for
\beta\in(1/4,1/2]
for finite
k
and
n
.
This completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Main lemmas used in error analysis of MLM

In this section, we collect the proofs of the main lemmas that are used in the error analysis of MLM (proof of Theorem
5
, provided in Section
6.3
).
We first introduce notation that is specific to these proofs.

For two indices
\ell,j\in[k]
, we use the Kronecker delta notation
\delta_{\ell,j}=\mathbb{I}[\ell\neq j]
.
For a diagonal covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
and
\ell\geq 1
, we define the leave-
\ell
-out covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell}
as
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
with the first
\ell
rows and columns removed.
For an arbitrary PSD matrix
\boldsymbol{M}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
with eigenvalues
{\lambda}_{1},\ldots,{\lambda}_{d}

and any index
k\in\{0,\ldots,d-1\}
, the effective rank of the first kind (in the sense of
[
BLLT20
]
) is defined as

\displaystyle r_{k}(\boldsymbol{M}):=\frac{1}{\lambda_{k+1}}\cdot\sum_{\ell=k+1}^{d}\lambda_{\ell}.

(77)

Additionally, we state our convention for constants for this proof.
Hereafter, we let
c,C\ldots>0
denote positive absolute constants in lower and upper bounds respectively. We also use
c_{k},C_{k}>0
in a similar manner to denote constants that may only depend on the number of classes
k
. To simplify exposition in the proof, the values of these constants may be changing from line to line without explicit reference.
Finally, by “large enough"
n
we mean that
n\geq C_{k}
for some universal constant
C_{k}
that depends only on
k
.

D.1
Proof of Lemma
4

The proof of Lemma
4
follows similarly to the proof of Theorem 4 in Appendix D.3 and Lemma 11 in Appendix E of
[
MNS+21
]
, with the two important and nontrivial extensions mentioned above: one, to the multiclass case involving
k
signal vectors, and two, considering the logistic model for label noise.
Without loss of generality
8
8

8

The reason this is without loss of generality is because we can carry out the same analysis otherwise with the appropriate permutation of the index labels.
, we assume for simplicity that
j_{c}=c
for all
c\in[k]
, and consider classes
c_{1}=1,c_{2}=2
for the argument.
First, we consider the following adjusted orthonormal basis

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\frac{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})}{\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\|_{2}},\qquad\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{2}=\frac{(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})}{\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\|_{2}},\qquad\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{j}=\boldsymbol{{e}}_{j}\text{ for all }j\geq 3.

This orthonormal basis together with the bilevel ensemble structure in Definition
4
then gives us

\displaystyle\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{H}}}{\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2}\|_{2}}\cdot(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{2})^{\top}(\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{1}-\widehat{\mathbf{w}}_{2})=\sqrt{\lambda_{H}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{2})

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\lambda_{H}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X})^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

where in the second line we introduce the shorthand
\mathbf{y}_{1}:=\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{2}\,
.
Next, we define

\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\mathbf{X}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\,,

with
\mathbf{z}_{j}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{j},j=1,\ldots,p
and note that
\mathbf{z}_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{iid}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,I_{n})
.
(This uses again the rotational invariance of Gaussianity
and the bilevel ensemble structure.) Finally, for
\ell=1,\ldots,p-1
, we denote the “leave-
\ell
-out” matrices
corresponding to the changed basis
by
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}:=\sum_{j=\ell+1}^{p}\lambda_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}
.
Note that, by definition,
\mathbf{A}_{-1:0}:=\mathbf{A}.

Using the above notation, we can then write the survival terms as follows

\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}=\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}.

The main challenge in characterizing the term above is that
\mathbf{A}^{-1}
is dependent on both
\mathbf{z}_{1}
and
\mathbf{y}_{1}
. In particular,
\mathbf{y}_{1}
depends on
\mathbf{z}_{1}
itself but also it depends on
\mathbf{z}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{k}
.
In the binary case, a simple
leave-one-out
analysis suffices to circumvent this difficulty as shown in
[
MNS+21
]
.
In the multiclass setting, we need to do a much more challenging
leave-
k
-out
analysis which we outline below.
In particular, we outline a recursive argument over
k
steps that iteratively removes the dependencies on
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{k}
from
\mathbf{A}^{-1}
. This process is described in the following subsections.

D.1.1
Key recursion: Removing dependencies

Start by defining the following “quadratic-like" terms:

\displaystyle Q_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1},\qquad\ell=0,\ldots,k\,,

(78a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1},\qquad\ell=1,\ldots,k\,,

(78b)

\displaystyle R_{\ell,j}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j},\qquad\ell\geq j,j=1,\ldots,k-1\,.

(78c)

Recall the term we wish to control is
Q_{0}=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:0}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}
.
A single application of the matrix inversion lemma (which was also done in
[
MNS+21
]
for the binary case and is described in a self-contained manner in Appendix
D.1.2
) yields that
\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}=\frac{\lambda_{H}Q_{1}}{1+\lambda_{H}\widetilde{Q}_{1}}
.
However, unlike in the binary case
Q_{1}
can no longer be easily controlled, as
\mathbf{y}_{1}
still depends on
\mathbf{A}_{-1:1}^{-1}
as it is a functional of not only
\mathbf{z}_{1}
, but also
\{\mathbf{z}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{k}\}
.

On the other hand, the term involving the leave-
k
-out Gram matrix, i.e.
Q_{k}=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}
avoids this issue.
This is because
\mathbf{y}_{1}
is only a functional of
\{\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{k}\}
, which ensures that
\mathbf{y}_{1}
is independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}
.
This allows us to sharply characterize
Q_{k}
via the Hanson-Wright inequality, as shown in the lemma below.

Lemma 15
.

For large enough
n
, we have

\displaystyle c_{k}\left(\frac{cn}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}+\frac{c^{\prime}n^{3/4}}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}\right)\geq Q_{k}

\displaystyle\geq c_{k}\left(\frac{(n-s)}{c\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}-\frac{c^{\prime}n^{3/4}}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}\right).

(79)

with probability at least
1-2e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

See Appendix
E.2.1
for the proof of this lemma.

Thus, it suffices to characterize
Q_{1}
in terms of
Q_{k}
, so that we can translate upper/lower bounds on
Q_{k}
to upper/lower bounds on
Q_{1}
and thereby characterize the survival
\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}
.
The main result of this section, shown below, does precisely this, guaranteeing that
|Q_{1}-Q_{k}|=o(Q_{k})
with high probability.

Lemma 16
.

We have

\left(1-\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\right)Q_{k}\leq Q_{1}\leq\left(1+\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\right)Q_{k}.

with probability at least
1-C^{\prime}k^{3}e^{-C\sqrt{n}}
.

In the remainder of this section we prove Lemma
16
.
We introduce the following recursion for any
\ell=k,\ldots,1
by directly applying the matrix inversion lemma:

\displaystyle Q_{\ell-1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell-1}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}+\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T})^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}=Q_{\ell}-\frac{{\lambda}_{H}(\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell})(\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell})}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}

\displaystyle=Q_{\ell}-\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}\,\left(\frac{\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}}\,\right)\left(\frac{{\lambda}_{H}R_{\ell,\ell}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}\right)\,,

(80)

where in the last line we recalled the definitions of
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}
and of
R_{\ell,\ell}
in Eqs. (
78
).

In order to prove Lemma
16
using the above recursion, we establish the following bounds on each of the three terms
\frac{\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}}
,
R_{\ell,\ell}
and
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}
that appear in Eq. (
D.1.1
). We provide the proofs of each of these technical lemmas in Appendix
E.2
.

Lemma 17
.

For large enough
n
and for all
\ell\in[k]
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1}=\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}\,,

(81)

with probability at least
1-Ck^{3}e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

Lemma 18
.

For all
\ell\in[k]
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert R_{\ell,\ell}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\,,\quad\ell=k,k-1,\ldots,1\,.

(82)

with probability at least
1-Ck^{3}e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

Lemma 19
.

For large enough
n
and for all
\ell\in[k]
, we have

\displaystyle 0\leq\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{c_{k}}Q_{k}\,

(83)

with probability at least
1-Cke^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

Proof of Lemma
16

Combining the bounds in Lemmas
17
,
18
,
19
and the fact that
\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\geq 0
within Equation (
D.1.1
) immediately yields for all
\ell=1,\ldots,k
:

\displaystyle\lvert Q_{\ell}-Q_{\ell-1}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{c_{k}}Q_{k}\,\left(\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\right)\,\left(\frac{C_{k}\cdot{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq Q_{k}\frac{C_{k}}{c_{k}n^{1/4}}.

The desired then follows by the bound
|Q_{k}-Q_{1}|\leq\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}|Q_{\ell}-Q_{\ell-1}|
.

D.1.2
Completing the proof of Lemma
4

Armed with Lemma
16
, we now complete the proof of Lemma
4
.
Recall that

\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}=\lambda_{H}Q_{0}=\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}\,.

Applying Equation (
D.1.1
) for
\ell=1
, we can write

\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}={\lambda}_{H}Q_{0}={\lambda}_{H}\left(Q_{1}-\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\widetilde{Q}_{1}\,Q_{1}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}{\lambda}_{H}\widetilde{Q}_{1}}\right)=\frac{{\lambda}_{H}Q_{1}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\widetilde{Q}_{1}}\,.

Thus, combining Lemmas
19
and
16
, we can obtain the following lower/upper bounds on
\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}
:

\displaystyle\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\left(1-\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\right)Q_{k}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\left(\frac{2}{c_{k}}\right)Q_{k}}\leq\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}\leq{\lambda}_{H}\left(1+\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\right)Q_{k}.

(84)

It remains to substitute the upper/lower bounds on
Q_{k}
we obtained in Lemma
15
.
Plugging in the definition of the bilevel ensemble gives
\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=n^{m}-n^{r}
.
Noting that
m>1
and
r<1
gives

\displaystyle\frac{cn}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}+\frac{c^{\prime}n^{3/4}}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}

\displaystyle\leq Cn^{1-m}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\frac{(n-s)}{c\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}-\frac{c^{\prime}n^{3/4}}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}

\displaystyle\geq cn^{1-m}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle cn^{1-m}\leq Q_{k}\leq Cn^{1-m}.

Noting that
\lambda_{H}=n^{m-q-r}
, we then have
cn^{1-q-r}\leq\lambda_{H}Q_{k}\leq Cn^{1-q-r}
.
Plugging this back into Equation (
84
) gives

\displaystyle c_{k}n^{1-q-r}\leq\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}\leq C_{k}n^{1-q-r}

for large enough
n
, which is the desired statement.
A union bound over Lemmas
15
and
16
implies that this statement holds with probability at least
1-Ck^{3}e^{-C\sqrt{n}}
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
4
.
∎

D.2
Proof of Lemma
5

This proof extends the argument in
[
MNS+21
, Proof of Theorem 24]
using the same change-of-basis argument that we used to characterize the survival.
As with the proof of Lemma
4
, we assume without loss of generality that
c_{1}=1,c_{2}=2
.
First, we recall that

\displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}:=\mathbf{X}(\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X})^{-1}(\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{2})=\mathbf{X}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1},

(85)

and that we defined
\mathbf{A}:=\mathbf{X}^{T}\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{y}_{1}:=\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{2}
as shorthand.
We first state and prove the following lemma, which is analogous to
[
MNS+21
, Lemma 28, Eq. (53a)]
.

Lemma 20
.

The contamination term
\mathsf{CN}_{1,2}
can be expressed as,

\displaystyle\mathsf{CN}_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{y}_{1}},\text{ where }

(86)

\displaystyle\mathbf{C}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{A}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}^{2}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\right)\mathbf{A}^{-1}.

This is a consequence of the relation
\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{1,2}:=\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}\hat{\alpha}_{j}^{2}
, where we define
\hat{\alpha}_{j}:=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{j}
.

See Appendix
E.3
for the proof of this lemma.

Note that the expression in Lemma
20
is still challenging to characterize, as the difference of label vectors
\mathbf{y}_{1}
is dependent on the matrix
\mathbf{C}
.
To make progress, we will write a
k
-step recursive equation to express
\hat{\alpha}_{j}
(and, thereby,
\mathsf{CN}_{1,2}
) in terms of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}
instead of
\mathbf{A}^{-1}
, leading to a possible characterization in terms of quadratic forms for which we can apply the Hanson-Wright inequality.
We begin by reproducing the first recursion from the proof of
[
MNS+21
, Lemma 28]
, which directly yields

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{j}=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:1}^{-1}(\mathbf{y}_{1}-\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}\mathbf{z}_{1}).

We now recurse this argument to get an expression in terms of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}
.
Applying the Sherman-Morrison formula yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{-1:1}^{-1}=\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}-\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{2}\mathbf{z}_{2}\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{2}}

and, consequently,

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{j}=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}\left(\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}-\mathbf{z}_{2}\cdot\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{2}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{2}}\right).

(87)

To write the entire
k
-step recursion, we define some shorthand notation.
For
\ell=2,\ldots,k
we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell-1}}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell}

\displaystyle:=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell-1}-\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\implies\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}_{1}-\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}.

Consequently, rewriting Equation (
87
) in terms of this shorthand notation gives

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{j}=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:2}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{2},

and repeating this argument for
\ell=3,\ldots,k
ultimately yields

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{j}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}.

Then, we use an identical set of manipulations to the proof of
[
MNS+21
, Lemma 28]
(reproduced for completeness) to get

\displaystyle\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{1,2}=\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}\hat{\alpha}_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}^{2}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}^{2}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\right)\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k},\text{ where }

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}^{2}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\right)\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}.

We now complete the proof of Lemma
5
by working with the expression
\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{1,2}=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}
.
First, we note that we can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}+\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}\text{ where }

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,1}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{k}\lambda_{j}^{2}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\right)\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}

\displaystyle:=\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\left(\sum_{j=k+1}^{d}\lambda_{j}^{2}\mathbf{z}_{j}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\right)\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}.

Then, we can sharply upper-bound the terms
T_{1}:=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}
and
T_{2}:=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}
, which we do below beginning with the second term
T_{2}
.

Controlling the term
T_{2}:=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}

We apply the algebraic identity
(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})^{\top}\mathbf{M}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y})\leq 2(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}+\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{y})

k-1
times to get

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}\leq 2^{k-1}\left(\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\mathbf{y}_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}(\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)})_{1,2}^{2}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\right).

We use the following technical lemma, which is proved in Appendix
E.3
.

Lemma 21
.

For
\ell=2,\ldots,k
we define

\displaystyle\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}:=\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell-1}}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}\,,

where
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell}:=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell-1}-\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}
and

\mathsf{SU}^{(1)}_{1,2}:=\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}=\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1}}.
Then, for all
\ell=2,\ldots,k
we have

\displaystyle\lvert\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}\rvert\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}<C_{k}\,.

(88)

with probability at least
1-Ck^{2}e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

Applying Lemma
21
thus gives

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}\leq C_{k}\left(\mathbf{y}_{1}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\mathbf{y}_{1}+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\right).

Now, we note that the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}
only depends on
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j=k+1}^{p}
and is therefore independent of
\mathbf{y}_{1}
as well as
\{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k}
.
Recall that each of
\{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{k}
is isotropic Gaussian and that
\mathbf{y}_{1}
is sub-Gaussian with uncorrelated components, i.e.
y_{1,i}^{2}\leq 1
and
\mathbb{E}[y_{1,i}y_{c,i^{\prime}}]=0
for
i\neq i^{\prime}\in[n]
.
Therefore, we can apply the Hanson-Wright inequality
[
RV+13
]
with the parameters stated in
[
MNS+21
, Eq (44)]
to get

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}\leq C_{k}\cdot\text{Tr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2})\cdot\log n

with probability at least
\left(1-\frac{1}{n}\right)
.
We denote by
\{\tilde{\lambda}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p-k}
the diagonal entries of the leave-
k
-out covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:k}
.
A direct application of
[
MNS+21
, Lemma 30]
(which, in turn, is taken from
[
BLLT20
, Lemma 11]
) gives

\displaystyle\text{Tr}(\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,2})

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\frac{s-k}{n}+n\cdot\frac{\sum_{j=s-k+1}^{p-k}\tilde{\lambda}_{j}^{2}}{(\sum_{j>s-k+1}^{p-k}\tilde{\lambda_{j}})^{2}}\right).

Then, substituting the bilevel ensemble parameterization in a manner identical to the proof of
[
MNS+21
, Lemma 35]
gives

\displaystyle T_{2}\leq C_{k}\cdot n^{-\min(m-1,2q+r-1)}\cdot\log n.

(89)

for
q>1-r
.

Controlling the term
T_{1}:=\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}

Unfortunately, this term is more delicate than
T_{2}
, because the matrix
\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,1}
intricately depends on
\mathbf{z}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{k}
.
However, we can unravel the expression back to get

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}^{\top}\widetilde{\mathbf{C}}_{k,1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=2}^{k}\lambda^{2}_{j}(\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{k})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=2}^{k}\lambda^{2}_{j}\left(|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}|+\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}|\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}||\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}|\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\sum_{j=2}^{k}\lambda^{2}_{j}\left(|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}|+|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1}|+\frac{1}{n^{1/4}}\sum_{\ell=2}^{k}|\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}|\right)^{2}

where the last inequality uses Lemma
21
and Lemma
4
.

The key observation is that there are only
O(k^{2})
such terms that we need to control.
Noting that
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}
is independent of each of
\mathbf{y}_{1}
and
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{k}
, we now use the Hanson-Wright inequality to control each of the terms
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}\}_{j=2}^{k}
and
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\}_{j\neq\ell}
.
Note that for
j=2,\ldots,k
, we have
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{y}_{1}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}]=\mathbf{0}
from Lemma
22
(a base technical lemma, proved in Appendix
E.1
) and
\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}]=\delta_{\ell,j}\mathbf{I}_{p}
.
We apply this inequality (as stated in
[
MNS+21
, Lemma 26]
) for the choice
t=\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot\sqrt{n\log n}
to get

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot\sqrt{n\log n}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\delta_{\ell,j}\cdot\text{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot\sqrt{n\log n},

each with probability at least
1-\frac{1}{n^{c}}
for some
c>0
.
Next, applying Lemma
23
(a base technical lemma, proved in Appendix
E.1
) gives us
\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\leq\frac{C}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
with probability at least
1-2e^{-\frac{n}{c}}
over the random matrix
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}
.
We further recall that
\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=n^{m}-n^{r}\geq cn^{m}
for large enough
n
, and that
\lambda_{j}=\lambda_{H}=n^{m-q-r}
for
j=2,\ldots,k
(because under our assumptions
s>k
).
Excluding the terms
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j=2}^{k}
for now, each of the above contributes the following to
T_{1}
:

\displaystyle\frac{C_{k}\cdot\lambda_{H}^{2}\cdot n\log n}{\lambda_{L}^{2}r_{s}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:k})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\cdot n^{1-2q-2r}\cdot\log n=:C_{k}\cdot n^{-(2q+2r-1)}\cdot\log n<n^{-(2q+r-1)},

which is identical to the scaling for
T_{2}
.
We finally return to controlling the terms
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j=2}^{k}
.
Note that each of these terms is pre-multiplied by the factor
\frac{1}{n^{1/4}}

Applying Lemma
23
again gives
\text{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})\leq\frac{Cn}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}
with probability at least
1-2e^{-\frac{n}{c}}
.
The contribution from each of these terms, thus, becomes

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}\frac{C_{k}\lambda_{H}^{2}n^{2}}{\lambda_{L}^{2}r_{s}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:k})}+\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}\frac{C_{k}\cdot\lambda_{H}^{2}\cdot n\log n}{\lambda_{L}^{2}r_{s}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:k})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n^{1/2}}\frac{C_{k}\lambda_{H}^{2}n^{2}}{\lambda_{L}^{2}r_{s}^{2}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:k})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\cdot n^{2-2q-2r-1/2}=C_{k}\cdot n^{-(2q+2r-3/2)}.

Thus, we get

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\cdot n^{-\min(2q+r-1,2q+2r-3/2)}\cdot\log n.

(90)

Putting it all together

Recall that
\mathsf{CN}_{1,2}^{2}:=T_{1}+T_{2}
.
Therefore, putting together the upper bounds from Equations (
90
) and (
89
) gives us the following statement:

\displaystyle\mathsf{CN}_{1,2}(n)\leq C_{k}\sqrt{\log n}\cdot n^{-\frac{\min\{m-1,2q+r-1,2q+2r-3/2\}}{2}}

for
q>1-r
and a universal constant
C_{k}
that depends only on
k
.
This is the desired statement.
Further, a union bound over each of the probabilistic inequalities implies that the statement holds with probability at least
1-\frac{C_{k}}{n^{c}}
for some
0<c\leq 1
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
5
.
∎

Appendix E
Supporting technical lemmas for MLM error analysis

In this section, we prove the supporting technical lemmas for the MLM error analysis.

E.1
Basic lemmas about the MLM

We begin by collecting basic lemmas about the MLM that form building blocks to prove the rest of the technical lemmas.
The first such basic lemma controls the expectation of certain product forms involving the difference label vector
\mathbf{y}_{1}
and individual feature vectors
\{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{p}
.

Lemma 22
.

Let
\mathbf{y}_{1}=\mathbf{v}_{1}-\mathbf{v}_{2}
be the difference label vector for
c_{1}=1,c_{2}=2
and
\{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\}_{\ell=1}^{p}
be defined as in the proof of Lemma
4
.
Then, we have for every
i\in[n]
,

\displaystyle c_{k,\ell}:=\mathbb{E}[y_{1,i}z_{\ell,i}]=c_{k}\delta_{1,\ell},

where
c_{k}>0
is a universal positive constant that depends only on
k
.

Proof.

To prove this lemma we utilize the orthogonality and equal-weight Assumption
5
as well as the details of the MLM.
We denote
\mathbf{u}_{j}:=\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{{e}}_{j}
.
It is easy to see from the definition of the changed basis
\{\mathbf{z}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p}
that
\mathbf{z}_{j}=\mathbf{u}_{j}
for all
j\geq 3
, and
\mathbf{z}_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{2})
and
\mathbf{z}_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\mathbf{u}_{1}+\mathbf{u}_{2})
.
We now use the simplex-ETF-type structure of
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2}
together with the structure in the MLM model to get

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(y_{1,i}=1\Big|\{u_{1,i},u_{2,i},\ldots,u_{k,i}\}\right)=\frac{\exp(u_{1,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{c^{\prime},i})}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(y_{1,i}=-1\Big|\{u_{1,i},u_{2,i},\ldots,u_{k,i}\right)=\frac{\exp(u_{2,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{j_{c^{\prime},i}})},

and
y_{1,i}=0
otherwise.
Note here that
\{u_{c,i}\}_{c\in[k]}
are i.i.d. standard Gaussian.
We start with the case
\ell=1
.
Here, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[z_{1,i}y_{1,i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[(u_{1,i}-u_{2,i})\cdot\frac{\exp(u_{1,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{c^{\prime},i})}-(u_{1,i}-u_{2,i})\cdot\frac{\exp(u_{2,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{j_{c^{\prime},i}})}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[(U_{1}-U_{2})\cdot\frac{(e^{U_{1}}-e^{U_{2}})}{\sum_{c=1}^{k}e^{U_{c}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[U_{1}\cdot\frac{(e^{U_{1}}-e^{U_{2}})}{\sum_{c=1}^{k}e^{U_{c}}}\right],

where the last step follows by symmetry.
Note that we have overloaded notation and written
U_{c}:=u_{c,i}
for each
c\in[k]
.
We also write
\mathbf{U}:=\begin{bmatrix}U_{1}&\ldots&U_{k}\end{bmatrix}
as shorthand.
Because
U_{c}\text{ i.i.d. }\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
, we have

\displaystyle c_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[U_{1}\cdot g(\mathbf{U})\right]

where
g(\mathbf{U}):=\frac{e^{U_{1}}-e^{U_{2}}}{\sum_{c=1}^{k}e^{U_{c}}}
.
Then, applying Stein’s lemma, we get

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[U_{1}\cdot g(\mathbf{U})\right]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}[U_{1}U_{i}]\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial g}{\partial U_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\partial g}{\partial U_{1}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\sum_{i\geq 3}e^{U_{1}+U_{i}}+2e^{U_{1}+U_{2}}}{(\sum_{i=1}^{k}e^{U_{i}})^{2}}\right]=:c_{k}>0.

The last step follows because the argument inside the expectation can never take value
0
and is always non-negative. Thus, we have proved that
\mathbb{E}[y_{1,i}z_{1,i}]=c_{k}>0
.

We now prove that
\mathbb{E}[y_{1,i}z_{\ell,i}]=0
for
\ell\neq 1
.
First, for
\ell\geq 3
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[y_{1,i}z_{\ell,i}]=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[u_{\ell,i}\cdot\frac{\exp(u_{1,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{c^{\prime},i})}-u_{\ell,i}\cdot\frac{\exp(u_{2,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{{c^{\prime},i}})}\right]=0

by symmetry.
Next, for
\ell=2
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[z_{2,i}y_{1,i}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[(u_{1,i}+u_{2,i})\cdot\frac{\exp(u_{1,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{c^{\prime},i})}-(u_{1,i}+u_{2,i})\cdot\frac{\exp(u_{2,i})}{\sum_{c^{\prime}\in[k]}\exp(u_{j_{c^{\prime},i}})}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[(U_{1}+U_{2})\cdot\frac{(e^{U_{1}}-e^{U_{2}})}{\sum_{c=1}^{k}e^{U_{c}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[U_{1}\cdot\frac{(e^{U_{1}}-e^{U_{2}})}{\sum_{c=1}^{k}e^{U_{c}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\left[U_{2}\cdot\frac{(e^{U_{2}}-e^{U_{1}})}{\sum_{c=1}^{k}e^{U_{c}}}\right]=0,

where the last equality follows by symmetry.
This completes the proof.
∎

The next basic lemma controls the trace and operator norm of leave-
\ell
-out Gram matrices and leverages ideas first appearing in
[
BLLT20
]
.

Lemma 23
.

For all
\ell\in[k]
and sufficiently large
n
, the following inequalities are true for universal constants
c,C>0
, each with probability at least
1-2e^{-\frac{n}{c}}
:

\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\leq\frac{c}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}\,

and

\frac{cn}{{\lambda}_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}\geq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})\geq\frac{(n-s)}{c{\lambda}_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}\,.

In particular, these imply

\displaystyle\frac{\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{1:\ell}^{-1})}\leq\frac{C_{2}}{n^{1/4}}\,.

(91)

Proof.

First, we upper bound the operator norm term. Observe that

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}=\mu_{1}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})=\frac{1}{\mu_{n}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell})}\leq\frac{1}{\mu_{n}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:s})}\leq\frac{c}{\lambda_{s+1}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})},

where the last inequality uses
[
BLLT20
, Lemma 5]
.
The second-to-last inequality holds for any choice of
s>k\geq\ell
.

Next, we prove the bounds for the trace term. We lower bound the trace term as

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\mu_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell})}\geq\sum_{j=s}^{n}\frac{1}{\mu_{j}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell})}\geq\frac{(n-s)}{\mu_{s+1}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell})}.

Thus, it remains to upper bound
\mu_{s+1}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell})
.
Let
\{\widetilde{\lambda}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{p-\ell}
denote the re-indexed eigenvalues of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell}
.
Then, Equation (38) from Lemma 25 in
[
MNS+21
]
directly yields

\displaystyle\mu_{s+1}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell})\leq C\widetilde{\lambda}_{s+1}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell})

provided that
r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell})\geq bn
.
(Note that, under the bilevel ensemble, we have
r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell})=\frac{n^{m}-\ell-s}{\lambda_{L}}\geq cn^{m}>bn
for large enough
n
.)
Similarly, we upper bound the trace term as

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})\leq\frac{n}{\mu_{n}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell})}\leq\frac{cn}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s+1}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell})}

where we now used Equation (37) from Lemma 25 in
[
MNS+21
]
.
To complete the proof for the trace term, we show that
\widetilde{\lambda}_{s+1}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell})\asymp\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
.
First, we note that
\widetilde{\lambda}_{s+1}=\lambda_{s+1}=\lambda_{L}
under the bilevel ensemble.
Also recall that
\ell\leq k<s
; hence we have
r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell})=p-s-\ell
and
r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})=p-s
, which implies that
r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}_{-1:\ell})\asymp r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})
for large enough
n
. Putting all of this together yields the desired inequalities about the trace.

Finally, we prove Equation (
91
).
This follows because, as already shown, we have

\displaystyle\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\leq\frac{c\,n^{3/4}}{\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{n-s}{c\,\lambda_{L}r_{s}(\boldsymbol{\Sigma})}.

thereby giving us

\displaystyle\frac{\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})}\leq\frac{n^{3/4}}{n-s}\leq\frac{2}{n^{1/4}}\,,

(92)

where the last inequality follows for large enough
n
because
s=n^{r}
and we have assumed
r<1
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

The following basic lemma relates the ratios of quadratic forms that are “similar" in their probability distribution.

Lemma 24
.

We have

\frac{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}}\leq C\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}}\leq C

for all
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in[k]
with probability at least
1-c\,k\,e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

Proof.

Recall that for any
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
, we have that
\mathbf{z}_{\ell},\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}
are both independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}

with probability at least
1-ke^{-\sqrt{n}}
.
Putting these together gives

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}\leq\frac{1+\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}{1-\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}\leq 2\,,

for large enough
n
, where in the above we used Eq. (
91
).

To prove the second inequality, recall that for any
\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in[k]
,
\mathbf{z}_{\ell}
is independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}
and
\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}
is independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}
.
Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}

with probability at least
1-ke^{-\sqrt{n}}
.
Putting these together gives

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell^{\prime}}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}\leq\frac{1+\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}{1-\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}\leq 2\,,

for large enough
n
, where we again used Eq. (
91
).
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Finally, the following basic lemma controls the ratio of traces of the leave-
\ell
-out Gram matrix and the leave-
k
-out Gram matrix for any
\ell\in[k]
.

Lemma 25
.

For all
\ell\in[k]
and sufficiently large
n
, it holds for universal constant
C
that

\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{1:k}^{-1})}\geq\left(1-\frac{C}{n}\right)^{k-\ell}\geq\left(1-\frac{C}{n}\right)^{k}

Proof.

Fix any
\ell\in[k]
.
We first lower-bound the ratio
\frac{\mathsf{tr}({\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}})}{\mathsf{tr}({\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}})}
, and then apply the argument recursively.
Since
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell)}=\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}
, we can apply the matrix inversion lemma to get

\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}=\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}-\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}\,.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}({\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1})-\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\,\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1})}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}=\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1})-\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\,\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-2}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}\,

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1})-\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\|_{2}\,\cdot\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\,\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1})-\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\|_{2}

(The second inequality follows because for any positive semidefinite matrix
\mathbf{M}
with eigendecomposition
\mathbf{M}=\mathbf{U}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}^{T}=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}^{T}
we have

\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{M}^{2}\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{x})^{T}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}^{2}(\mathbf{U}\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}^{2}(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{x})^{2}\leq\left(\max_{i}\lambda_{i}\right)\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}(\mathbf{u}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{x})^{2}=\|\mathbf{M}\|_{2}\cdot\mathbf{x}^{T}\mathbf{M}\mathbf{x}\,

for any vector
\mathbf{x}
.)
Continuing from the penultimate display, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathsf{tr}({\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}})}{\mathsf{tr}({\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}})}\geq 1-\frac{\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\|_{2}}{{\mathsf{tr}({\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}})}}\geq 1-\frac{C}{n}

where the last inequality applies Eq. (
91
).
Recursively applying the above for
\ell+1,\ldots,k
completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

E.2
Survival Term

In this section we provide the proofs of Lemmas
15
,
17
,
18
and
19
.

E.2.1
Proof of Lemma
15

First, we note that
\mathbf{y}_{1}
remains independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}
as
\mathbf{y}_{1}
only depends on
\mathbf{z}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{k}
(which are in turn mutually independent of
\mathbf{z}_{k+1},\ldots,\mathbf{z}_{p}
which comprise of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}
).
Therefore, we can directly apply the Hanson-Wright inequality to get

\displaystyle Q_{k}

\displaystyle\geq c_{k}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\text{ and }

(93a)

\displaystyle Q_{k}

\displaystyle\leq c_{k}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}.

(93b)

with probability at least
1-2e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.
Combining the above with Lemma
23
applied for
\ell=k
directly gives the desired statement of Equation (
79
), completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

E.2.2
Proof of Lemma
17

Note that the quadratic-like terms in both the LHS and RHS of (
81
) are well-suited for an application of the Hanson-Wright inequality, since
\mathbf{z}_{1},\mathbf{z}_{\ell}
are independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}
for all
\ell=2,\ldots,k
. This is formalized in the lemma below. Specifically, the desired statement to prove Lemma
17
, i.e. Eq. (
81
) for
\ell=1,\ldots,k
, follows directly by applying Lemma
26
below for the special case
\ell^{\prime}=j=1
.
(The slightly more general statement of the lemma below will prove useful for proving subsequent lemmas.)

Lemma 26
.

For large enough
n
, for all
\ell\in[k]
and
\ell^{\prime}<\ell,j\leq\ell
we have

\lvert\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rvert\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\,\mathbf{z}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}\,.

with probability at least
1-Ck^{3}e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

Proof.

The key observation is that for all
\ell^{\prime}<\ell,j\leq\ell
, we have that
\mathbf{z}_{\ell},\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}},
and
\mathbf{z}_{j}
are all mutually independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}
.
Therefore, applying the Hanson-Wright inequality in the form stated by
[
MNS+21
]
gives us the following: for all
\ell\in[k],\ell^{\prime}<\ell,j\leq\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\qquad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}

\displaystyle\geq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\,,

with probability at least
1-Ck^{3}e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.
Above, we used the fact that
\mathbf{z}_{\ell},\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}
are independent. Therefore, to prove the desired it suffices to show that

\displaystyle{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{1:\ell}^{-1})}\geq\frac{n^{1/4}}{C_{2}}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\,.

(94)

This follows immediately from Eq. (
91
) in Lemma
23
.
This completes the proof.
∎

E.2.3
Proof of Lemma
18

Recall that
R_{\ell,\ell}:=\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}.
Bounding this term is difficult because
\mathbf{y}_{1}
depends on
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}
for any
\ell<k
