. The only “easy” case is for
\ell=k
for which
\mathbf{y}_{1}
is independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}
.
As a starting point, we exploit this independence to control the terms
R_{k,\ell}=\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}
for all
\ell\in[k]
, in the lemma below.

Lemma 27
.

We have, for large enough
n
,

\lvert R_{k,\ell}\rvert=\lvert\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\rvert\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\,\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\ \text{ for any }\ell=2,\ldots,k\,,

and

\lvert R_{k,1}\rvert=\lvert\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1}\rvert\leq C_{k}\,\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\,

with probability at least
1-Cke^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

Proof.

Recall that all of
\mathbf{y}_{1},\mathbf{z}_{\ell},\mathbf{z}_{k}
are independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}
.
Therefore, we can apply the Hanson-Wright inequality to the RHS of the above, as well as
R_{k,\ell}
(using the parallelogram law in the latter case) to get

\displaystyle\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\leq\mathbf{z}_{k}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}

\displaystyle\leq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}

\displaystyle c_{k,\ell}\cdot\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}\leq R_{k,\ell}

\displaystyle\leq c_{k,\ell}\cdot\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4},

with probability at least
1-Cke^{-\sqrt{n}}
.
Above, we define
c_{k,\ell}:=\mathbb{E}[y_{1,i}z_{\ell,i}]
(identically for any
i\in[n]
).
There are then two cases:

1.

\ell=1
: In this case we get
c_{k,\ell}=:c_{k}>0
from Lemma
22
.
Plugging this above gives

\displaystyle\frac{R_{k,1}}{\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c_{k}\cdot\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{k}\frac{1+\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}{1-\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}\leq 2c_{k}=:C_{k},

where the second inequality follows from Eq. (
91
) in Lemma
23
and the last inequality follows for large enough
n
.
Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\frac{R_{k,1}}{\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2c_{1}}{\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})}{\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}+c_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2c_{1}}{\frac{n^{1/4}}{2}+c_{1}}\geq-\frac{C}{n^{1/4}},

where the second-to-last inequality in the above again used Equation (
91
).

2.

\ell\neq 1
: In this case we have
c_{k,\ell}=0
, again from Lemma
22
.
Plugging this above gives

\displaystyle\frac{R_{k,\ell}}{\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2c_{1}}{\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})}{\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}+c_{1}}\leq\frac{2c_{1}}{\frac{n^{1/4}}{2}+c_{1}}\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/4}},

where the last inequality follows for large enough
n
.
Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\frac{R_{k,\ell}}{\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{2c_{1}}{\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})}{\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}+c_{1}}

\displaystyle\geq-\frac{2c_{1}}{\frac{n^{1/4}}{2}+c_{1}}\geq-\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}

where in the penultimate line we again used Eq. (
91
).

∎

We now build on the “base case" Lemma
27
to control the terms
R_{\ell,\ell}
in a similar manner to
R_{k,\ell}
.
In particular, we note that the desired Eq. (
82
) to prove Lemma
18
follows by applying the slightly more general lemma below for the case
\ell^{\prime}=\ell
.

Lemma 28
.

For all
\ell\in[k]
and all
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert R_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\rvert\leq\begin{cases}C_{k}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\ \text{ if }\ell^{\prime}=1\\
\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\ \text{ if }\ell^{\prime}\neq 1\end{cases}

(95)

with probability at least
1-ck^{3}e^{-\sqrt{n}}
.

We complete the proof of Lemma
18
by proving Lemma
28
, which we do in the next section.

E.2.4
Proof of Lemma
28

We will use recursion starting from
\ell=k
to prove the desired statement for all
\ell=k-1,k-2,\ldots,1
. Throughout, we condition on the events of Lemmas
24
,
26
, and
27
. The key to allow proving the statement recursively is the following relation that follows by the matrix-inversion-lemma and holds for all
\ell^{\prime}\leq\ell
:

\displaystyle R_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}=\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\left(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}+\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}-\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\left(\mathbf{y}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\left(\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}

\displaystyle=R_{\ell+1,\ell^{\prime}}-R_{\ell+1,\ell+1}\,\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\left(\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}\,.

(96)

First we prove the statement for the base case
\ell=k-1
. For any
\ell^{\prime}\leq k-1
, Equation (
96
) gives us

\displaystyle R_{k-1,\ell^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=R_{k,\ell^{\prime}}-R_{k,k}\,\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\left(\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}}\,.

Note that because
\ell^{\prime}\leq k-1
, we have
\ell^{\prime}<k
.
Thus, we can apply Lemma
26
to get

\displaystyle\lvert\epsilon_{k,\ell^{\prime}}\rvert:=\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\lvert\left(\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\rvert}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}}\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\,\frac{{\lambda}_{H}{\left(\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\right)}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}}\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\,.

Also, by Lemma
27
, we have

\displaystyle\lvert R_{k,1}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\lvert R_{k,j}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\text{ for all }j=2,\ldots,k.

Combining the three displays above with the recursion in Equation (
96
) yields the following for large enough
n
:

\displaystyle\lvert R_{k-1,1}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\left(1+Cn^{-1/4}\right)\cdot\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\leq C_{k}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\lvert R_{k-1,\ell^{\prime}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}n^{-1/4}\left(1+C\right)\cdot\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k}\text{ for all }\ell^{\prime}\in\{2,\ldots,k-1\}.

Lemma
24
(applied for the pair (
k,k-1
)) then gives us the desired Equation (
95
) for
\ell=k-1
, i.e.
\lvert R_{k-1,1}\rvert\leq C_{k}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{k-1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k-1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k-1}
and
\lvert R_{k-1,\ell^{\prime}}\rvert\leq C_{k}n^{-1/4}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{k-1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k-1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{k-1}
for
\ell^{\prime}=2,\ldots,k-1
.
The base case is therefore proved.

Next, we prove the inductive step. In particular, we assume that Equation (
95
) is true for
\ell+1
and use it to prove the claim for
\ell
. Our starting point is, again, the recursive relation in Equation (
96
).
Noting that
\ell^{\prime}<\ell+1
, we can again apply Lemma
26
to get

\displaystyle\lvert\epsilon_{\ell+1,\ell^{\prime}}\rvert:=\frac{{\lambda}_{H}\lvert\left(\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell^{\prime}}\right)\rvert}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\,\frac{{\lambda}_{H}{\left(\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\right)}}{1+{\lambda}_{H}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}}\leq\frac{C}{n^{1/4}}\,.

Also, by the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle\lvert R_{\ell+1,1}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\,\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\text{ and }

\displaystyle\lvert R_{\ell+1,j}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\,\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\text{ for all }j=2,\ldots,k.

Note that the sharper second inequality above applies to the term
R_{\ell+1,\ell+1}
because we always have
\ell+1\geq 2
.
Combining the two displays above with the recursion in Equation (
96
) yields the following for large enough
n
:

\displaystyle\lvert R_{\ell,1}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\left(1+Cn^{-1/4}\right)\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\leq C_{k}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\leq C_{k}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}

Similarly, we have for all
\ell^{\prime}=2,\ldots,\ell
,

\displaystyle\lvert R_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}n^{-1/4}\left(1+C\right)\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\leq C_{k}n^{-1/4}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell+1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell+1}\leq C_{k}n^{-1/4}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}

In both cases above, the last inequality follows from Lemma
24
.
This completes the proof of Lemma
28
, and therefore the proof of Lemma
18
.
∎

E.2.5
Proof of Lemma
19

Recall the definitions

Q_{k}:=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}\quad\text{and}\quad\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}:=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1}.

Since
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}
is a quadratic form, we have
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}\geq 0
and so it suffices to upper bound
\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}
.
Because
\mathbf{z}_{1}
is independent of
\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}
for any
\ell=1,\ldots,k
, we can directly apply the Hanson-Wright inequality to get

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}=\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1}\leq\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}

with probability
1-Ce^{-\sqrt{n}}
.
Similarly, applying the Hanson-Wright inequality to the term
Q_{k}
(see Eq.(
93
))
we also have

\displaystyle Q_{k}\geq c_{k}\cdot\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}.

with the same probability.
Putting these together, we get

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{Q}_{\ell}}{Q_{k}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{1:\ell}^{-1})+c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}{c_{k}\cdot\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})-2c_{1}\|\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1}\|_{2}\cdot n^{3/4}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{-1:k}^{-1})}{\mathsf{tr}(\mathbf{A}_{1:\ell}^{-1})}\,\frac{1+\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}{c_{k}-\frac{2c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(1-\frac{C}{n}\right)^{-k}\,\left(\frac{1+\frac{c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}{c_{k}-\frac{2c_{1}}{n^{1/4}}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{c_{k}},

where the second inequality follows from Lemma
23
for
\ell
and
k
(for large enough
n
) and the second-to-last inequality uses Lemma
25
. The last inequality follows again assuming large enough
n
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

E.3
Contamination Term

In this section we prove Lemmas
20
and
21
.

E.3.1
Proof of Lemma
20

First, we note that the desired Equation (
86
) is a direct consequence of the expression
\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{1,2}:=\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}\hat{\alpha}_{j}^{2}
, where

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{j}:=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{j}.

Therefore, it suffices to show that
\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{c_{1},c_{2}}=\sum_{j=1,j\neq 1}^{d}\lambda_{j}\hat{\alpha}_{j}^{2}
.

We denote the error vector
\boldsymbol{\xi}:=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}-\frac{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}\|_{2}^{2}}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}
as shorthand, and recall from Lemma
3
that we have
\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{1,2}=\boldsymbol{\xi}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\xi}
.
Further, we define
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}\\
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{2}\\
\vdots\\
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{d}\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
as the changed basis in matrix form, and we define
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}:=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\boldsymbol{\xi}
.
Then, the desired follows from these two statements:

1.

We have
\mathsf{CN}^{2}_{1,2}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}
.

2.

We have
\tilde{\xi}_{1}=0
and
\tilde{\xi}_{j}=\hat{\alpha}_{j}
for all
j=2,\ldots,d
.

We complete the proof by proving statements
1
and
2
for the specific form of
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
admitted by the bilevel ensemble.

Proof of statement 1

We prove this statement for a generic vector
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Consider the vector
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}:=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\mathbf{y}
. We will show that
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}=\mathbf{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\mathbf{y}
.
Because
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}
is a diagonal matrix, we have
\mathbf{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\mathbf{y}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}y_{j}^{2}
.
Further, it is straightforward to show from the specific form of the changed basis
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}
that
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{1}=\frac{y_{1}-y_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}
,
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{2}=\frac{y_{1}+y_{2}}{\sqrt{2}}
, and
\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j}=y_{j}
for
j=3,\ldots,d
.
Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}\tilde{y}_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}\tilde{y}_{2}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{H}(\tilde{y}_{1}^{2}+\tilde{y}_{2}^{2})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{H}\left(\frac{y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}-2y_{1}y_{2}+y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2}+2y_{1}y_{2}}{2}\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda_{H}(y_{1}^{2}+y_{2}^{2})=\lambda_{1}y_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}y_{2}^{2}.

Consequently, we have

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{y}}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}\tilde{y}_{j}^{2}=\lambda_{1}\tilde{y}_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}\tilde{y}_{2}^{2}+\sum_{j=3}^{d}\lambda_{j}\tilde{y}_{j}^{2}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}y_{1}^{2}+\lambda_{2}y_{2}^{2}+\sum_{j=3}^{d}\lambda_{j}y_{j}^{2}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\lambda_{j}y_{j}^{2}=\mathbf{y}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\mathbf{y}.

Hence, we have proved statement
1
.

Proof of statement 2

First, note that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{\xi}}=\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}-\frac{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}
.
Recall that
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}\propto\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}
, and so,
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}\propto\boldsymbol{{e}}_{1}
.
Next, simple algebra shows that

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2})_{j}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{{e}}_{j}^{\top}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{j}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}=\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{X}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}^{-1}\mathbf{y}_{1}=:\hat{\alpha}_{j}.

where the third equality recalls the definition of
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}
from Equation (
85
) and the second-to-last equality recalls the definition
\mathbf{z}_{j}:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\mathbf{X}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{j}
.
Noting that, by definition,
(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2})_{j}=0
for all
j\neq 1
, we have thus shown that
\tilde{\xi}_{j}=\hat{\alpha}_{j}
for all
j=2,\ldots,d
.
To complete the proof of statement
2
, we need to show that
\tilde{\xi}_{1}=0
.
Denote
\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}=\alpha\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}
for some
\alpha>0
(as a consequence, we also have
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}=\alpha\boldsymbol{{e}}_{1}
).
Then, it is equivalent to show that

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\alpha=\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}^{\top}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}.

(Recall that
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{E}}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}\propto\boldsymbol{{e}}_{1}
, so this equality suffices to show the desired.)
Starting with the LHS of the above, we get

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}\alpha\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{1}^{1/2}\alpha\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}.

Therefore, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}\|_{2}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1},\text{ and }

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}{\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1/2}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{1,2}}\|_{2}^{2}}\cdot\alpha

\displaystyle=\widehat{\boldsymbol{\Delta}}_{1,2}^{\top}\boldsymbol{\widetilde{e}}_{1}.

This completes the proof of statement
2
.

With statements
1
and
2
proved, the proof of this lemma is complete.
∎

E.3.2
Proof of Lemma
21

We prove the lemma using induction on
\ell=1,\ldots,k
. For the base case
\ell=1
, we have shown in Lemma
4
that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathsf{SU}^{(1)}_{1,2}\rvert:=\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\lvert\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:1}^{-1}{\mathbf{y}}_{1}\rvert}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{1}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:1}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{1}}=\lvert\mathsf{SU}_{1,2}\rvert\leq C_{k}\,.

(97)

Now, we prove the inductive step. We fix
\ell>1
and assume, along with the base case (Equation (
97
)), that the statement is also true for
2,\ldots,\ell-1
, i.e.

\displaystyle\forall j=2,\ldots,\ell-1,\qquad\lvert\mathsf{SU}^{(j)}_{1,2}\rvert

\displaystyle:=\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\lvert\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:j}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{j-1}\rvert}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:j}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}<C_{k}.

(98)

(In fact, as we will see, we will only need to apply the weaker inequality
\lvert\mathsf{SU}^{(j)}_{1,2}\rvert\leq C_{k}
.)
We use Equation (
98
) to prove the desired statement for
\ell
.
Consider first the numerator in the definition of
\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}
, i.e. the term

\displaystyle\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell-1}=\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\left(\mathbf{y}_{1}-\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\mathsf{SU}^{(j)}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\mathbf{z}_{j}\right)=\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}{\mathbf{y}}_{1}-\sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}\mathsf{SU}^{(j)}_{c_{1},c_{2}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}

Recall that
\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}{\mathbf{y}}_{1}=R_{\ell,\ell}
.
Note that Lemma
18
shows for
\ell\geq 2
that

\lvert R_{\ell,\ell}\rvert\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\,,

Also recall from Lemma
26
that for all
j<\ell
, we have

\lvert\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}\rvert\leq Cn^{-1/4}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{j}\leq Cn^{-1/4}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\,,

where again, the second inequality uses Lemma
24
.

Putting the above together, applying the triangle inequality and using the induction hypothesis (i.e.
\lvert\mathsf{SU}^{(j)}_{1,2}\rvert\leq C_{k}
) we conclude that

\displaystyle\lvert\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell-1}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\left(C_{k}\cdot n^{-1/4}+\ell\cdot C_{k}\cdot n^{-1/4}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\,.

(99)

This gives us

\displaystyle\lvert\mathsf{SU}^{(\ell)}_{1,2}\rvert

\displaystyle:=\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot\lvert\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\tilde{\mathbf{y}}_{\ell-1}\rvert}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}\leq\frac{1}{n^{1/4}}\cdot\frac{\lambda_{H}\cdot C_{k}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}{1+\lambda_{H}\cdot\mathbf{z}_{\ell}^{\top}\mathbf{A}_{-1:\ell}^{-1}\mathbf{z}_{\ell}}\leq\frac{C_{k}}{n^{1/4}}\,

(100)

for all
\ell\geq 2
.
This completes the proof of the lemma.

Appendix F
Recursive formulas for higher-order quadratic forms

We first show how quadratic forms involving the
j
-th order Gram matrix
\mathbf{A}_{j}^{-1}
can be expressed using quadratic forms involving the
(j-1)
-th order Gram matrix
\mathbf{A}_{j-1}^{-1}
. For concreteness, we consider
j=1
; identical expressions hold for any
j>1
with the only change being in the superscripts.
Recall from Section
6.2
that we can write

\displaystyle\mathbf{A}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{A}_{0}+\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}&\mathbf{Q}^{T}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}&\mathbf{v}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}^{T}\mathbf{Q}\end{bmatrix}=\mathbf{Q}^{T}\mathbf{Q}+\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}&\mathbf{d}_{1}&\mathbf{v}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{d}_{1}^{T}\end{bmatrix}.

The first step is to derive an expression for
\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}
. By the Woodbury identity
[
HJ12
]
, we get

\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}=\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}-\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}&\mathbf{d}_{1}&\mathbf{v}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}+\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{d}_{1}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}&\mathbf{d}_{1}&\mathbf{v}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{d}_{1}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}.

(101)

We first compute the inverse of the
3\times 3
matrix
\mathbf{B}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{I}+\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{T}\\
\mathbf{d}_{1}^{T}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}\mathbf{v}_{1}&\mathbf{d}_{1}&\mathbf{v}_{1}\end{bmatrix}\end{bmatrix}
.
Recalling our definitions of the terms
s_{mj}^{(c)},h_{mj}^{(c)}
and
t_{mj}^{(c)}
in Equation (
40
) in Section
6.2
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbf{B}=\begin{bmatrix}1+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{11}^{(0)}&\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{11}^{(0)}&\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{11}^{(0)}\\
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{11}^{(0)}&1+h_{11}^{(0)}&s_{11}^{(0)}\\
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{11}^{(0)}&t_{11}^{(0)}&1+h_{11}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}.

Recalling
\mathbf{B}^{-1}=\frac{1}{\det_{0}}\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})
, where
\det_{0}
is the determinant of
\mathbf{B}
and
\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})
is the adjoint of
\mathbf{B}
, simple algebra gives us

\displaystyle\text{det}_{0}=s_{11}^{(0)}(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)})+(h_{11}^{(0)}+1)^{2},

and

\displaystyle\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})=\begin{bmatrix}(h_{11}^{(0)}+1)^{2}-s_{11}^{(0)}t_{11}^{(0)}&\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}(s_{11}^{(0)}t_{11}^{(0)}-h_{11}^{(0)}-{h_{11}^{(0)}}^{2})&-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{11}^{(0)}\\
-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{11}^{(0)}&h_{11}^{(0)}+1+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{11}^{(0)}&-s_{11}^{(0)}\\
\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}(s_{11}^{(0)}t_{11}^{(0)}-h_{11}^{(0)}-{h_{11}^{(0)}}^{2})&\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}{h_{11}^{(0)}}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)}(1+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{11}^{(0)})&h_{11}^{(0)}+1+\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}s_{11}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}.

We will now use these expressions to derive expressions for the
1
-order quadratic forms that are used in Appendix
A.2
.

F.1
Expressions for 1-st order quadratic forms

We now show how quadratic forms of order
1
can be expressed as a function of quadratic forms of order
0
.
All of the expressions are derived as a consequence of plugging in the expression for
\mathbf{B}^{-1}
together with elementary matrix algebra.

First, we have

\displaystyle s_{mk}^{(1)}=\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{k}-\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{m1}^{(0)}&h_{m1}^{(0)}&s_{m1}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}\frac{\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})}{\det_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{k1}^{(0)}\\
s_{k1}^{(0)}\\
h_{k1}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=s_{mk}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{\det_{0}}(\star)_{s}^{(0)},

(102)

where we define

\displaystyle(\star)_{s}^{(0)}

\displaystyle:=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)})s_{1k}^{(0)}s_{1m}^{(0)}+s_{1m}^{(0)}h_{k1}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}+s_{1k}^{(0)}h_{m1}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}-s_{11}^{(0)}h_{k1}^{(0)}h_{m1}^{(0)}+s_{1m}^{(0)}h_{k1}^{(0)}+s_{1k}^{(0)}h_{m1}^{(0)}.

Thus, for the case
m=k
we have

\displaystyle s_{kk}^{(1)}=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{v}_{k}-\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{k1}^{(0)}&h_{k1}^{(0)}&s_{k1}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}\frac{\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})}{\det_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{k1}^{(0)}\\
s_{k1}^{(0)}\\
h_{k1}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=s_{kk}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{\det_{0}}\Big((\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)}){s_{1k}^{(0)}}^{2}+2s_{1k}^{(0)}{h_{k1}^{(0)}}h_{11}^{(0)}-s_{11}^{(0)}{h_{k1}^{(0)}}^{2}+2s_{1k}^{(0)}h_{k1}^{(0)}\Big).

(103)

Next, we have

\displaystyle h_{mk}^{(1)}=\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{m}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{k}-\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{m1}^{(0)}&h_{m1}^{(0)}&s_{m1}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}\frac{\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})}{\det_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{1k}^{(0)}\\
h_{1k}^{(0)}\\
t_{1k}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=h_{mk}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{\det_{0}}(\star)_{h}^{(0)},

(104)

where we define

(\star)_{h}^{(0)}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)})s_{1m}^{(0)}h_{1k}^{(0)}+h_{m1}^{(0)}h_{1k}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}+h_{m1}^{(0)}h_{1k}^{(0)}+s_{1m}^{(0)}t_{k1}^{(0)}+s_{1m}^{(0)}t_{k1}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}-s_{11}^{(0)}t_{k1}^{(0)}h_{m1}^{(0)}.

Next, we have

\displaystyle t_{km}^{(1)}=\mathbf{d}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{m}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{m}-\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{1k}^{(0)}&t_{1k}^{(0)}&h_{1k}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}\frac{\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})}{\det_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{1m}^{(0)}\\
h_{1m}^{(0)}\\
t_{1m}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=t_{km}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{\det_{0}}(\star)_{t}^{(0)},

(105)

where we define

(\star)_{t}^{(0)}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)})h_{1m}^{(0)}h_{1k}^{(0)}+t_{m1}^{(0)}h_{1k}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}+t_{k1}^{(0)}h_{1m}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}+t_{1m}^{(0)}h_{1k}^{(0)}+t_{1k}^{(0)}h_{1m}^{(0)}-s_{11}^{(0)}t_{1m}^{(0)}t_{1k}^{(0)}.

Thus, for the case
m=k
we have

\displaystyle t_{kk}^{(1)}=\mathbf{d}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{d}_{k}-\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{1k}^{(0)}&t_{1k}^{(0)}&h_{1k}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}\frac{\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})}{\det_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{1k}^{(0)}\\
h_{1k}^{(0)}\\
t_{1k}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=t_{kk}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{\det_{0}}\Big((\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)}){h_{1k}^{(0)}}^{2}+2t_{1k}^{(0)}{h_{1k}^{(0)}}h_{11}^{(0)}-s_{11}^{(0)}{t_{1k}^{(0)}}^{2}+2t_{1k}^{(0)}h_{1k}^{(0)}\Big).

(106)

Next, we have

\displaystyle f_{ki}^{(1)}=\mathbf{d}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{d}_{k}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{i}-\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}h_{1k}^{(0)}&t_{1k}^{(0)}&h_{1k}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}\frac{\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})}{\det_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}g_{1i}^{(0)}\\
g_{1i}^{(0)}\\
f_{1i}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=f_{ki}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{\det_{0}}(\star)_{f}^{(0)},

(107)

where we define

(\star)_{f}^{(0)}=(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)})h_{1k}^{(0)}g_{1i}^{(0)}+t_{1k}^{(0)}g_{1i}^{(0)}+t_{1k}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}g_{1i}^{(0)}+h_{1k}^{(0)}f_{1i}^{(0)}+h_{1k}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}f_{1i}^{(0)}-s_{11}^{(0)}t_{1k}^{(0)}f_{1i}^{(0)}.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle g_{ji}^{(1)}=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{1}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{v}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{A}_{0}^{-1}\mathbf{e}_{i}-\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}s_{j1}^{(0)}&h_{j1}^{(0)}&s_{j1}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}\frac{\text{adj}(\mathbf{B})}{\det_{0}}\begin{bmatrix}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}}\|_{2}g_{1i}^{(0)}\\
g_{1i}^{(0)}\\
f_{1i}^{(0)}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=g_{ji}^{(0)}-\frac{1}{\det_{0}}(\star)_{gj}^{(0)},

(108)

where we define

(\star)_{gj}^{(0)}=(\|\boldsymbol{\mu}_{1}\|_{2}^{2}-t_{11}^{(0)})s_{1j}^{(0)}g_{1i}^{(0)}+g_{1i}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}h_{j1}^{(0)}+g_{1i}^{(0)}h_{j1}^{(0)}+s_{1j}^{(0)}f_{1i}^{(0)}+s_{1j}^{(0)}h_{11}^{(0)}f_{1i}^{(0)}-s_{11}^{(0)}h_{j1}^{(0)}f_{1i}^{(0)}.

∎

Appendix G
One-vs-all SVM

In this section, we derive conditions under which the OvA solutions
\mathbf{w}_{{\rm OvA},c}
interpolate, i.e, all data points are support vectors in Equation (
8
).

G.1
Gaussian mixture model

As in the case of the multiclass SVM, we assume nearly equal priors and nearly equal energy on the class means (Assumption
1
).

Theorem 7
.

Assume that the training set follows a multiclass GMM with noise covariance
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
and Assumption
1
holds. Then, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>1
and
C_{1},C_{2}>1
such that the solutions of the OvA-SVM and MNI are identical with probability at least
1-\frac{c_{1}}{n}-c_{2}ke^{-\frac{n}{c_{3}k^{2}}}

provided that

\displaystyle p>C_{1}kn\log(kn)+n-1\quad\text{ and }\quad p>C_{2}n^{1.5}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}.

(109)

We can compare Equation (
109
) with the corresponding condition for multiclass SVM in Theorem
2
(Equation (
16
)).
Observe that the right-hand-side of Equation (
109
) above does not scale with
k
, while the right-hand-side of Equation (
16
) scales with
k
as
k^{3}
. Otherwise, the scalings with
n
and energy of class means
\|\boldsymbol{\mu}\|_{2}
are identical.
This discrepancy with respect to
k
-dependence arises because the multiclass SVM is equivalent to the OvA-SVM in Equation (
34
) with unequal margins
1/k
and
(k-1)/k
(as we showed in Theorem
1
).

Proof sketch.

Recall from Section
6.2
that we derived conditions under which the multiclass SVM interpolates the training data by studying the related symmetric OvA-type classifier defined in Equation (
15
). Thus, this proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
2
provided in Section
6.2
.
The only difference is that the margins for the OvA-SVM are not
1/k
and
(k-1)/k
, but
1
for all classes.
Owing to the similarity between the arguments, we restrict ourselves to a proof sketch here.

Following Section
6.2
and Equation (
46
), we consider
y_{i}=k
.
We will derive conditions under which the condition

\displaystyle\big((1+h_{kk}^{(-k)})g_{ki}^{(-k)}-s_{kk}^{(-k)}f_{ki}^{(-k)}\big)+C\sum_{j\neq k}\big((1+h_{jj}^{(-j)})g_{ji}^{(-j)}-s_{jj}^{(-j)}f_{ji}^{(-j)}\big)>0,

(110)

holds with high probability for some
C>1
.
We define

\displaystyle\epsilon:=\frac{n^{1.5}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}}{p}\leq\tau,

where
\tau
is chosen to be a sufficiently small constant. Applying the same trick as in Lemma
2
(with the newly defined parameters
\epsilon
and
\tau
) gives us with probability at least
1-\frac{c_{1}}{n}-c_{2}ke^{-\frac{n}{c_{3}k^{2}}}
:

(
110
)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\left(1-\frac{C_{1}\epsilon}{\sqrt{k}\sqrt{n}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{C_{2}}\right)\frac{1}{p}-\frac{C_{3}\epsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{n}{kp}\right)-\frac{k}{C_{4}}\left(\left(1+\frac{C_{5}\epsilon}{\sqrt{k}\sqrt{n}}\right)\frac{1}{kp}-\frac{C_{6}\epsilon}{n}\cdot\frac{n}{kp}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-{\frac{1}{C_{9}}}-\frac{C_{10}\epsilon}{\sqrt{k}\sqrt{n}}-\frac{C_{11}\epsilon}{{k}}-C_{12}\epsilon\right)\frac{1}{p}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{p}\left(1-\frac{1}{C_{9}}-C_{0}\tau\right),

(111)

for some constants
C_{i}
’s
>1
.
We used the fact that
|g_{ji}^{(0)}|\leq(1/C)(1/(kp))
for
j\neq y_{i}
with probability at least
1-\frac{c_{1}}{n}-c_{2}ke^{-\frac{n}{c_{3}k^{2}}}
provided that
p>C_{1}kn\log(kn)+n-1
, which is the first sufficient condition in the theorem statement.
∎

G.2
Multinomial logistic model

Recall that we defined the data covariance matrix
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\sum_{i=1}^{p}\lambda_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{T}=\boldsymbol{V}\boldsymbol{\Lambda}\boldsymbol{V}^{T}
and its spectrum
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\begin{bmatrix}\lambda_{1}&\cdots&\lambda_{p}\end{bmatrix}
.
We also defined the effective dimensions
d_{2}:=\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{1}^{2}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{2}^{2}}
and
d_{\infty}:=\frac{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{1}}{\|\boldsymbol{\lambda}\|_{\infty}}
.

The following result provides sufficient conditions under which the OvA SVM and MNI classifier have the same solution with high probability under the MLM.

Theorem 8
.

Assume that the training set follows a multiclass MLM. There exist constants
c
and
C_{1},C_{2}>1
such that, if the following conditions hold:

\displaystyle d_{\infty}>C_{1}n\log(kn)~~\text{and}~~d_{2}>C_{2}(\log(kn)+n),

(112)

the solutions of the OvA-SVM and MNI are identical with probability at least
(1-\frac{c}{n})
.
In the special case of isotropic covariance, the same result holds provided that

\displaystyle p>10n\log(\sqrt{k}n)+n-1,

(113)

Comparing this result to the corresponding results in Theorems
3
, we observe that
k
now only appears in the
\log
function (as a result of
k
union bounds).
Thus, the unequal
1/k
and
(k-1)/k
margins that appear in the multiclass-SVM make interpolation harder than with the OvA-SVM, just as in the GMM case.

Proof sketch.

For the OvA SVM classifier, we need to solve
k
binary max-margin classification problems, hence the proof follows directly from
[
MNS+21
, Theorem 1]
and
[
HMX21
, Theorem 1]
by applying
k
union bounds. We omit the details for brevity.
∎

One-vs-one SVM

In this section, we first derive conditions under which the OvO solutions interpolate, i.e, all data points are support vectors. We then provide an upper bound on the classification error of the OvO solution.

In OvO classification, we solve
k(k-1)/2
binary classification problems, e.g. for classes pair
(c,j)
, we solve

\displaystyle\mathbf{w}_{{\rm OvO},(c,j)}:=\arg\min_{\mathbf{w}}\|{\mathbf{w}}\|_{2}\quad\text{sub. to}~~\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{i}\geq 1,~\text{if}~\mathbf{y}_{i}=c;~~\mathbf{w}^{T}\mathbf{x}_{i}\leq-1~\text{if}~\mathbf{y}_{i}=j,~\forall i\in[n].

(114)

Then we apply these
k(k-1)/2
classifiers to a fresh sample and the class that got the highest
+1
voting gets predicted.

We now present conditions under which every data point becomes a support vector over these
k(k-1)/2
problems. We again assume nearly equal priors and nearly equal energy on the class means (Assumption
1
).

Theorem 9
.

Assume that the training set follows a multiclass GMM with noise covariance
\boldsymbol{\Sigma}=\mathbf{I}_{p}
and Assumption
1
holds. Then, there exist constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}>1
and
C_{1},C_{2}>1
such that the solutions of the OvA-SVM and MNI are identical with probability at least
1-\frac{c_{1}}{n}-c_{2}ke^{-\frac{n}{c_{3}k^{2}}}

provided that

\displaystyle p>C_{1}n\log(kn)+(2n/k)-1\quad\text{ and }\quad p>C_{2}n^{1.5}\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}.

(115)

Proof sketch.

Note that the margins of OvO SVM are
1
and
-1
, hence the proof is similar to the proof of Theorem
7
. Recall that in OvO SVM, we solve
k(k-1)/2
binary problems and each problems has sample size
2n/k
with high probability. Therefore, compared to OvA SVM which solves
k
problems each with sample size
n
, OvO SVM needs less overparameterization to achieve interpolation. Thus the first condition in Equation (
109
) reduces to
p>C_{1}n\log(kn)+(2n/k)-1
.
∎

We now derive the classification risk for OvO SVM classifiers. Recall that OvO classification solves
k(k-1)/2
binary subproblems. Specifically, for each pair of classes, say
(i,j)\in[k]\times[k]
, we train a classifier
\mathbf{w}_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
and the corresponding decision rule for a fresh sample
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
is
\hat{y}_{ij}=\text{sign}(\mathbf{x}^{T}\hat{\mathbf{w}}_{ij})
. Overall, each class
i\in[k]
gets a voting score
s_{i}=\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{1}_{\hat{y}_{ij}=+1}
. Thus, the final decision is given by majority rule that
decides the class with the highest score
, i.e.
\arg\max_{i\in[k]}s_{i}
. Having described the classification process, the total classification error
\mathbb{P}_{e}
for balanced classes is given by the conditional error
\mathbb{P}_{e|c}
given the fresh sample belongs to class
c
. Without loss of generality, we assume
c=1
. Formally,
\mathbb{P}_{e}=\mathbb{P}_{e|1}=\mathbb{P}_{e|1}(s_{1}<s_{2}~\text{or}~s_{1}<s_{3}~\text{or}\cdots\text{or}~s_{1}<s_{k})
. Under the nearly equal prior and energy assumption, by symmetry and union bound, the conditional classification risk given that true class is
1
can be upper bounded as:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e|1}(s_{1}<s_{2}~\text{or}~s_{1}<s_{3}~\text{or}\cdots\text{or}~s_{1}<s_{k})\leq\mathbb{P}_{e|1}(s_{1}<k-1)=\mathbb{P}_{e|1}(\exists j~s.t.~\hat{y}_{1j}\neq 1)\leq(k-1)\mathbb{P}_{e|1}(\hat{y}_{12}\neq 1).

Therefore, it suffices to bound
\mathbb{P}_{e|1}(y_{12}\neq 1)
. We can directly apply Theorem
4
with changing
k
to
2
and
n
to
2n/k
.

Theorem 10
.

Let Assumptions
1
and
2
, and the condition in Equation (
115
) hold. Further assume constants
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>1
such that
\big(1-C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}-\frac{C_{2}n}{kp}\big)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}>C_{3}.
Then, there exist additional constants
c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}
and
C_{4}>1
such that the OvO SVM solutions satisfies:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{e|c}\leq(k-1)\exp{\left(-\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}\frac{\left(\left(1-C_{1}\sqrt{\frac{k}{n}}-\frac{C_{2}n}{kp}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}-C_{3}\right)^{2}}{C_{4}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{kp}{n}\right)}\right)}

(116)

with probability at least
1-\frac{c_{1}}{n}-c_{2}ke^{-\frac{n}{c_{3}k^{2}}}
, for every
c\in[k]
.
Moreover, the same bound holds for the total classification error
\mathbb{P}_{e}
.
